# Claims ledger: the three argued Honest Answers (unit apol)

Date: 2026-11-18. Every claim of fact, date, name, title and quotation in the three rewritten answers in `client/src/data/answers.ts` (`does-god-actually-exist`, `why-would-anyone-trust-the-bible`, `if-god-is-good-why-suffering`), with how it was checked.

"Essay ledger" means the claim already appears in a public essay whose claims ledger under `docs/work/essays/ledgers/` records it as verified; the claim is reused in the same wording or a plain paraphrase of it. "Search" means a WebSearch run in this unit (27 searches in all). Where a search confirmed a fact but not a person's exact wording, the answer gives it as attributed paraphrase, without quotation marks, and the line says so. Scripture is ESV; each verse quoted was already quoted in a verified essay ledger or answer, except where noted.

# Second run (2026-11-18): faith and science, the resurrection, hell

Same method as above. "Search" is a WebSearch run in this second run (12 at most per answer). "Repo" means the wording is already quoted on the site in a study guide or answer.

## Seventh run (2026-11-19): repair and three answers

### Repair: "Did God command genocide in the Old Testament?"

Two more searches for the 2009 *Philosophia Christi* articles (titles exact). The articles' own text was not reached: pdcnet.org, spot.colorado.edu (Morriston's own page, which hosts a later paper), and blog.epsociety.org do not resolve from this session. One search result describes Rauser's abstract as concluding that God did not command the Canaanite genocide. Nothing in the results gives Morriston's or Rauser's premises in their own words: Morriston's argument appears only through a blog reconstruction (Philosophical Disquisitions, 2010) and the introduction to his later *Sophia* article (2012); Rauser's syllogism only through a course slide. So three sentences were reworded: the articles are named as where the objection is argued ("pressed it there"; Rauser "concluded that God did not give these commands"); the objection is stated "in my words and not theirs"; the laid-out argument is labeled "my own summary of the objection argued in the 2009 symposium"; and "Morriston argued that a Christian ... can't easily deny the first premise" became "A Christian ... can't easily deny the first premise, so on this summary the dispute comes down to the second." The rows above are updated.

# Tenth run (2026-11-20): the pilot area "Can we trust the text of the New Testament?", and the library map

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/` (index.md; more-variants-than-words.md, "a"; copies-of-copies.md, "b"; passages-added.md, "c"; orthodox-corruption.md, "d"). Plan: `docs/work/elite/apol-library.md`. Method words as above. "WTB ledger" means `docs/work/essays/ledgers/why-trust-the-bible.md`, three closing runs and a final read. "S" is a WebSearch run in this run (43 in all); the search tool returns summaries of pages, so a "verified" row below rests on secondary pages (Wikipedia, publishers, reviews, journal records) unless it says otherwise. "Repo" is a file in this repository. "Note" means a Study Bible note in `client/public/bible/notes/` that the pilot agrees with; no fact rests on a note alone. No direct fetch of any page worked (biblehub.com failed to resolve).

# Eleventh run (2026-11-20): five more pieces in the text area

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/` and `client/public/questions/text/`: compared-with-other-ancient-books.md, "e"; who-decides-what-the-text-says.md, "f"; king-james-and-the-missing-verses.md, "g"; lost-in-translation.md, "h"; an-angry-jesus-mark-1-41.md, "i". "S" is a WebSearch run in this run; the search tool returns summaries of pages, so "verified (secondary)" means a search summary of Wikipedia, a publisher, a review, a catalogue or a blog, not the primary text. "Tenth run" means a row in the section above; "WTB ledger" means `docs/work/essays/ledgers/why-trust-the-bible.md`; "Repo" means a file in this repository (the BSB text in `client/public/bible/text/`, the Study Bible notes in `client/public/bible/notes/`). Direct fetches failed: ehrmanblog.org and www.gutenberg.org do not resolve from this machine, so no primary text was read. No book was read; summaries of arguments rest on reviews, tables of contents, publishers' descriptions and the authors' own summaries as reported, and are written as paraphrase.

# Thirteenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the authors area, and the science area

Method words as in the twelfth run. "S" is a WebSearch run in this run (summaries of pages, so "verified (secondary)" means a search summary, not the primary text); "Twelfth run" means the rows above; "Repo" a file in this repository; "Note" a Study Bible introduction. No book was read from this machine.

## The editor's read of the five authors pieces (changed facts and wording)

Read twice: as a hostile, well-read skeptic, then as a careful conservative scholar. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data; `diff-questions-record.mjs` shows 0 differences. The five stay pending, and the area stays pending.

| Piece | Before | After | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| j | "belong to men of no standing" (short answer) | "belong to men who were not among the Twelve" | Overclaim: Mark and Luke are named in Acts and in Paul's letters with standing in the churches; the argument needs only that they were not apostles |
| j | "There are no early copies ... under other names, and none that circulated with a blank where the name should be." | "There are no early copies of these four Gospels under other names." | How copies circulated can't be known from what survives; Kruger's verified point (twelfth run, j) concerns copies that keep a title page, which the paragraph already states |
| j | "The church has always confessed Hebrews as Scripture without knowing who wrote it" | "The church received Hebrews as Scripture without knowing who wrote it" | "Always" overclaimed: Hebrews was long doubted in parts of the church; the narrower sentence needs no new fact |
| k | "They were written decades later, on any dating" | "On the dating most scholars hold, they were written decades later" | Logic: the same piece cites Crossley's date for Mark in the late 30s or early 40s, under a decade after the crucifixion |
| k | "Matthew and Luke in the 80s or 90s" (short answer) | "Matthew and Luke between about 75 and 95" | Agrees with the piece's own evidence section and the Note on Luke (majority view 75 to 90) |
| k | Step 1: "On any dating scholars defend ... thirty-five and sixty-five years ..., and on the minority view some were written sooner." | "On the dating most scholars hold ... thirty-five and sixty-five years ...; a minority dates some of them sooner, and a few date Luke and Acts later." | The old step contradicted itself and left out Pervo's date for Acts (about 115), which the piece cites |
| k | "Ignatius of Antioch and the Didache, early in the second century, appear to know Matthew." | "Ignatius of Antioch, around 110 on the usual dating, and the Didache appear to know Matthew." | The twelfth-run row says "on the usual dating" was kept for Ignatius, but the text lacked it; the Didache's date is argued (some place it in the first century). Note (Matthew intro): "the Didache and Ignatius of Antioch (around 110)" |
| k | Arrian "written about four centuries after" Alexander's campaigns | "more than four centuries after" | Arithmetic: the campaigns 334 to 323 BC, Arrian in the second century AD; the gap is about four and a half centuries |
| k | "His critics have pressed hard. Samuel Byrskog ... wrote of the eyewitnesses as interpreters of the past, shaping what they saw as they told it. Others have challenged the statistics of the names." | "His critics have pressed hard, and the statistics of the names have been challenged. Samuel Byrskog's response in the *JSHJ* (2008) is titled "The Eyewitnesses as Interpreters of the Past," and the title names the issue: a witness interprets what he saw as well as remembering it." | The twelfth run verified only the article's title, and cut any description of Byrskog's stance; the old text placed him among hard critics and gave him an argument not seen. Now only the title is used, and its point is offered as the title's |
| k | "A tradition being told in churches where those men still preached and visited couldn't drift freely." | "A tradition told in churches where those men had preached, and where people who had heard them were still alive, wasn't free to drift wherever a community wished." | Peter and Paul died in the 60s, before most of the Gospels on the majority dates; "still preached" was wrong for the time of writing, and "couldn't" overclaimed |
| l | Bauckham "explained in the same book that eyewitness memory can be very unreliable" | "has said plainly that eyewitness memory can be very unreliable" | Twelfth run row k verified the statement from a 2015 blog post and an interview, not from the book |
| l | "traditions he gathered first-hand in Middle Eastern communities" | "traditions he gathered himself" | Repetition (the sentence already says Middle Eastern village communities); no fact changed |
| m | "churches from Gaul to Egypt were reading them in worship and calling them the only four" (short answer) | "churches from Gaul to Egypt held them as Scripture, and Irenaeus could call them the only four" | Only Irenaeus is verified as saying "only four"; worship reading is verified for Rome (Justin), not for Egypt |
| m | "None of these four has a serious claim ... and only Thomas has serious defenders for an early date for part of its contents." | "None of these four is earlier than the four in the New Testament on the usual scholarly dating, though some scholars argue that parts of Thomas preserve earlier tradition." | "Only" was not verified (some scholars have argued for an early source behind the Gospel of Peter); the narrower sentence states what the twelfth run verified |
| m | Step 1: "read in worship as Scripture across the churches" | "received as Scripture across the churches" | As above |
| m | "the four were never in dispute among them" | "the four are not in dispute among them" | The sentence is about churches now; "never" invited a historical claim not checked |
| n | "Every article of the creeds is taught in letters no one doubts." (short answer) | "What the creeds confess about Christ is taught in letters almost no one doubts, and in the Gospels." | Overclaim: some articles (the virgin birth among them) are taught in the Gospels, not the undisputed letters; step 3 already said "and in the Gospels"; "no one" became "almost no one" (a few have doubted all of Paul's letters), as in step 3 |
| n | "attested in 1 Corinthians and Galatians, letters no one doubts" and "in the letters no one doubts" | "letters almost no one doubts", "in the letters almost no one doubts" | As above |
| n | "Second Thessalonians follows 1 Thessalonians phrase by phrase" | "follows 1 Thessalonians closely in its wording" | Twelfth run row: "follows 1 Thessalonians closely"; "phrase by phrase" overstated the objection's own evidence |
| n | Richards "using the letters of Cicero and his secretary Tiro among his evidence" | "drawing on Cicero's letters and his use of secretaries among his evidence" | Twelfth run row: "draws on Cicero and his secretaries"; Tiro was not seen in the sources |
| n | "Critics answer that the differences Richards documents are smaller than those between Ephesians and Romans" | "One critic has answered that the shifts of style Richards documents are far smaller than Ephesians or Colossians would need" | Twelfth run row: one critic, and the comparison is with what Ephesians or Colossians would need |
| n | "The church of the second century rejected letters it judged forged, as the Muratorian fragment shows" | "The early church rejected writings it judged forged, as Tertullian's story and the Muratorian fragment show" | The fragment's date is argued (traditionally late second century, Hahneman fourth); Tertullian (about 200) is the firmer witness |
| n | Wilder: "early Christians had scruples that rule such a practice out" | "ancient writers, early Christians among them, had scruples about writing under another's name, which tells against it" | Twelfth run row: "had scruples about pseudonymity"; "rule out" overstated |

Checked and left: every name, date, title and journal in the five against its twelfth-run row; each piece against the Study Bible introductions (Matthew, Mark, Luke, John, Acts, Colossians and the others it names: Ignatius around 110, Luke 75 to 90 majority, Dunn on Colossians "with Paul's approval", Davies and Allison on Matthew all agree) and the Honest Answers (no conflict found). No living author is quoted in the five. The objections were reread for their defenders' signature: Ehrman's case in j, k and n, Bultmann's in l and Bauer's and Ehrman's in m are stated in their own terms and kept.

## The area "science" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/science/` and `client/public/questions/science/`: the area page index.md; has-science-made-god-unnecessary.md, "o"; galileo-and-the-war.md, "p"; miracles-and-the-laws-of-nature.md, "q"; the-beginning-and-fine-tuning.md, "r"; evolution-and-genesis.md, "s". "Second run", "Fifth run", "Ninth run" and "God answer" mean the rows above (faith and science, /faq/can-science-and-faith-coexist, miracles, Genesis 1, "Does God actually exist?"). "Hume" means the Selby-Bigge text of *An Enquiry Concerning Human Understanding* (Project Gutenberg #9662), cloned again in this run from GITenberg on GitHub and searched with grep; every Hume quotation below was read there word for word. Scripture is quoted from the site's Berean Standard Bible (`client/public/bible/text/`), marked "BSB" in the text, and read in the file before it was quoted. The site takes no position on the age of the earth, evolution, a historical Adam's details or the flood's extent.

# Fourteenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the science area, and the area "The historical Jesus and the resurrection"

Method words as in the thirteenth run. "S" is a WebSearch run in this run (summaries of pages, so "verified (secondary)" means a search summary, not the primary text). "Read" means a primary text read word for word in a public-domain edition cloned from GITenberg (Project Gutenberg's books on GitHub, the only source host this machine reaches; gutenberg.org, Wikipedia, Wikisource, newadvent.org, ccel.org, archive.org, perseus.tufts.edu and penelope.uchicago.edu do not resolve): "Summa" is the *Summa Theologica*, Part I, translated by the Fathers of the English Dominican Province (Gutenberg #17611); "von Gebler" is Karl von Gebler, *Galileo Galilei and the Roman Curia*, translated by Jane Sturge (1879; Gutenberg #60215), which prints the 1616 censure in Latin and English and the 1633 sentence in English. "Repo" means a file in this repository.

## The editor's read of the five science pieces (changed facts and wording)

Read three times: as a working scientist and historian of science hostile to apologetics, then as a young-earth, an old-earth and an evolutionary-creationist reader in turn. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data; `diff-questions-record.mjs` shows 0 differences. The five and the area stay pending.

| Piece | Before | After | Why, and how verified |
|---|---|---|---|
| o | Aquinas's objection quoted "in a common English translation" | "in the English Dominican translation" | Read: Summa I q.2 a.3, Obj. 2 ("Therefore there is no need to suppose God's existence.") and Reply Obj. 2 ("whatever is done by nature must needs be traced back to God, as to its first cause") are word for word in the English Dominican translation. The thirteenth run had them only from search results. Also read: I q.105 a.5 (God the cause of action in every agent without taking away the created power), which the piece paraphrases |
| o | Plantinga quoted: "superficial conflict but deep concord" and "superficial concord but deep conflict" | Paraphrased: the conflict between science and belief in God is shallow and the agreement deep; between science and naturalism the agreement is shallow and the conflict deep | Plantinga is a living author and the second-run row rests on reviews quoting p. ix, not the book; the brief allows no quotation of a living author not seen in the primary text |
| p | "vehemently suspect of heresy" (twice, short answer and concessions) | "vehemently suspected of heresy" | Read: von Gebler, the sentence of 22 June 1633, "have rendered yourself in the judgment of this Holy Office vehemently suspected of heresy". The old wording matched no translation seen |

No living author is now quoted in the five (Dawkins, Coyne, Krauss, Rees, Albert, Venema, Wood, Meyer, Behe, Haarsma, Keener, Earman, Fogelin and Plantinga are paraphrased). The six readings of Genesis were reread for their defenders' signature and for their difficulties: each states its own strongest point and its hardest difficulty, unranked. Miracles (q) needed no change: its Hume quotations were read word for word in the thirteenth run.

## The area "jesus" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/jesus/` and `client/public/questions/jesus/`: the area page index.md; did-jesus-exist.md, "t"; the-empty-tomb.md, "u"; the-appearances-and-the-alternatives.md, "v"; the-resurrection-accounts-disagree.md, "w"; can-historians-affirm-a-miracle.md, "x". "HJ ledger" means `docs/work/essays/ledgers/the-historical-jesus.md` and "Res ledger" `docs/work/essays/ledgers/did-the-resurrection-happen.md` (both with three closing passes and a final read, several primary texts read from GitHub clones); "Res answer" means the rows above under "Did the resurrection actually happen?"; "Note" means a Study Bible chapter note in `client/public/bible/notes/`. Primary texts read word for word in this run, all public domain, cloned from GitHub: "Whiston" (Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews* and *The Wars of the Jews*, translated by William Whiston, Gutenberg #2848 and #2850); "Church and Brodribb" (Tacitus, *Annals*, Perseus `phi1351.phi005.perseus-eng2.xml`, its header naming Alfred John Church and William Jackson Brodribb, Macmillan 1869); "Melmoth" (Pliny, *Letters*, translated by William Melmoth, revised by F. C. T. Bosanquet, Gutenberg #2811, where 10.96 is numbered XCVII); "Thomson" (Suetonius, *Claudius* and *Nero*, translated by Alexander Thomson, revised by T. Forester, Gutenberg #6390 and #6391); "Crombie" (Origen, *Against Celsus*, ANF translation by Frederick Crombie, Gutenberg #70561). Scripture is quoted from the site's Berean Standard Bible (`client/public/bible/text/`), marked BSB, every quotation checked by script. The resurrection is stated as the church's confession; the historical argument is offered with its limits. No percentage or head-count of scholars is given anywhere in the area.

# Fifteenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the jesus area, and the area "Contradictions and hard passages"

Method words as in the fourteenth run. "S" is a WebSearch run in this run (summaries of pages, so "verified (secondary)" means a search summary, not the primary text). "GH" means a file read in full from a public GitHub repository, the only source host this machine reaches (europepmc.org, pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov, bmj.com, en.wikipedia.org and archive.org do not resolve); "SEP miracles" is the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy's entry "Miracles" (Timothy McGrew), read in a copy kept on GitHub (`tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/Miracles.txt`); "Rees abstract" is the abstract of W. Dewi Rees, "The Hallucinations of Widowhood," *BMJ* 4 (1971): 37-41, as quoted word for word in a research note on GitHub (`unfinishablemap/unfinishablemap`, `obsidian/research/voids-bereavement-void-2026-11-16.md`), which also gives the modality figures from Kamp and others' table. "Repo" means a file in this repository; "Note" a Study Bible chapter note.

## The editor's read of the five jesus pieces (changed facts and wording)

Read three times: as a skeptical ancient historian, as a Jewish reader alert to any line that could feed blame of the Jewish people, and as a careful conservative scholar. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area stay pending.

Brenton's English Septuagint, 1844, `codexcommunion/bible-toolkit`, chapter files); "Chicago" (the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy, 1978, `pmachapman/Confessions/chicago-statement.html`). "Hebrew" means the site's own Hebrew text, `client/public/bible/ch/`, which is STEPBible's TAHOT ("The Leningrad codex based on Westminster via OpenScriptures", STEPBible-Data README on GitHub), cross-checked against the TAHOT file itself. Scripture in English is quoted from the site's BSB, checked by script. "Note" means a Study Bible chapter note or book introduction in this repository, whose wording each piece agrees with. The site takes no side between inerrancy and infallibility and does not state James's view.

# Sixteenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the contradictions area, and the area "The Old Testament: history, archaeology, violence and law"

Method words as in the fourteenth and fifteenth runs. "S" is a WebSearch run in this run (summaries of pages: "verified (secondary)"). "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, still the only source host this machine reaches (ccel.org does not resolve). "Chicago" is the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy (1978), fetched again this run from `pmachapman/Confessions/chicago-statement.html` and read: article 10 (affirmation: "inspiration, strictly speaking, applies only to the autographic text of Scripture ... copies and translations of Scripture are the Word of God to the extent that they faithfully represent the original"), article 11 (denial: "Infallibility and inerrancy may be distinguished, but not separated."), article 13 (denial: "a lack of modern technical precision ... the use of hyperbole and round numbers, the topical arrangement of material, variant selections of material in parallel accounts, or the use of free citations"), article 14 (denial: "We deny that alleged errors and discrepancies that have not yet been resolved vitiate the truth claims of the Bible."). Every quotation of the Statement in the five pieces matches, with the article number right. "Repo", "Note" and "BSB" as before.

## The editor's read of the five contradictions pieces and the area page (changed facts and wording)

Read three times: as a skeptic who knows these texts, as an inerrantist scholar, and as a scholar who holds Scripture infallible in what God gave it to teach. Each asked whether his own view is put in words he would sign and his difficulty honestly named. The pages take no side among views of Scripture's truthfulness and nowhere state James's view (searched). Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area stay pending.

(short answer) | "On the evidence historians can weigh, he may have, and an honest answer has to say so." | Inerrantist read: in the site's own voice the bare "He may have" took a side against inerrancy; the frame now says what kind of judgment it is. The skeptic still hears his point granted |

| ac | Inerrancy: "differences arising in copying do not touch that confession" | adds "and that the rest can be harmonized or await an explanation" | Inerrantist read: the old sentence gave his view only for copying errors, though the page grants that some differences may not be copying slips |
| ac | (checked and left) Japhet, *I & II Chronicles* (OTL, Westminster John Knox, 1993); Williamson, *1 and 2 Chronicles* (New Century Bible, Eerdmans, 1982); Thiele, *The Mysterious Numbers of the Hebrew Kings* (1951) | unchanged | S: catalogue records and *JHS* citations (Japhet, 1993; Williamson, Eerdmans 1982); library catalogue records (Tübingen KEIBI; DAI Zenon) and Strand, *AUSS* 1996: Thiele's first edition University of Chicago Press, 1951, later editions 1965 and 1983. Verified (secondary) |
| Area page | Infallibility: "as used here, the conviction that Scripture never fails in what God gave it to teach, the way of salvation and of life with him." | adds "Christians who hold to inerrancy confess Scripture infallible too; the Chicago Statement says the two 'may be distinguished, but not separated.'" | Inerrantist read: the area used "infallibility" for one view only, though inerrantists confess it too. Chicago, article 11, read this run |

Checked and left: every BSB quotation in the five by script (one reference, Mark 14:12 with 16, covers two verses; checked by hand: "they prepared the Passover" is 14:16); the Nicene Creed's "spake by the prophets" (Repo); Papias, Augustine, Eusebius, Justin, Paine and Josephus quotations (read word for word in the fifteenth run); the Judas page names no means beyond Matthew 27:5, quoted once, and Acts 1:18-19, which describes the fall; it says nothing of Judas's eternal state or of anyone else who has died by suicide. Africanus's own date for his letter was not found; nothing in the pages depends on it beyond the century.

## The area "old-testament" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/old-testament/` and `client/public/questions/old-testament/`: the area page index.md; did-the-exodus-happen.md, "ad"; jericho-and-the-conquest.md, "ae"; the-command-to-destroy-the-canaanites.md, "af"; when-was-daniel-written.md, "ag"; the-flood-and-babylon.md, "ah". "Note" means a Study Bible chapter note or book introduction in this repository (`client/public/bible/notes/`), whose wording each piece agrees with; "WOT ledger" means `docs/work/elite/wot-ledger.md` (the Old Testament world portrait, its rows verified by search); "Bdoor ledger" `docs/work/elite/bdoor-ledger.md`; "Genocide answer" the rows above under "Did God command genocide in the Old Testament?" and the essay ledger `docs/work/essays/ledgers/did-god-command-genocide.md` they rest on. Primary texts read word for word in this run from GitHub copies, all public domain: "CE 1909" (the *Catholic Encyclopedia*, vol. 5, "Egypt", in `gustavo-depaula/ember`, which prints a translation of the Merneptah inscription); "Spillan" (Livy, *History of Rome*, books 1 to 8, literally translated by D. Spillan, Gutenberg #19725 in GITenberg); "Budge" (E. A. Wallis Budge, *The Babylonian Story of the Deluge and the Epic of Gilgamish*, British Museum, revised by H. R. Hall, 1929, the sacred-texts copy in `jonnyg23/mahabrain`, `ane/gilgdelu.md`); "Jerome" (Jerome, *Commentary on Daniel*, prologue and chapter 11, translated by Gleason L. Archer, 1958, transcribed by Roger Pearse and marked public domain, in `HistoricalChristianFaith/Writings-Database`; the prologue's Latin checked in `eulogikon/ancient-greek-texts` and the *Patrologia* OCR); "Whiston" (Josephus, *Antiquities*, Gutenberg #2848, the clone from the fourteenth run); "KJV Apocrypha" (1 Maccabees and Sirach in the King James Version, `typiconman/ponomar`, `languages/en/bible/kjv/`). Scripture in English is quoted from the site's BSB and checked by script. No living author is quoted. No consensus is stated as a percentage or head-count.

# Seventeenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the old-testament area, and the area "Evil, suffering and judgment"

Method words as in the fourteenth to sixteenth runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, found with GitHub's code search; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches (gutenberg.org, archive.org, en.wikipedia.org, plato.stanford.edu, jstor.org, philpapers.org, ccel.org, doi.org, arxiv.org and journals.plos.org were each tried with curl this run and none resolved). No WebSearch was run in this run, so nothing below rests on a search summary that was not already in an earlier row. "SEP" means an entry of the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy read in full in the copy kept on GitHub (`tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/<title>.txt`, the copy the fifteenth run used for "Miracles"). "Wikipedia copy" means an English Wikipedia article read in a copy on GitHub; it is a tertiary source, used only for plain facts of date and publication and said to be such. "Note", "Repo" and "BSB" as before.

## The editor's read of the five old-testament pieces and the area page (changed facts and wording)

Read four times: as an archaeologist skeptical of apologetics; as a Jewish reader (these are Israel's Scriptures: no line may read as contempt for Judaism, and the Canaanite page must never read as a warrant against any people or modern state, nor mention any modern conflict; it mentions none); as a conservative scholar holding an early exodus and a sixth-century Daniel; and as a scholar holding the late dates. Each asked whether his own view is put in words he would sign. The sources the sixteenth run marked secondary were rechecked one by one; where no primary or reliable accessible source could be opened, the claim was narrowed to what the Study Bible's notes already state, or cut. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0), and the two claim lines that depended on cut wording were changed with them. All five and the area stay pending.

## The area "evil" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/evil/` and `client/public/questions/evil/`: the area page index.md; the-logical-problem-of-evil.md, "ai"; the-evidential-problem-of-evil.md, "aj"; natural-evil-and-animal-suffering.md, "ak"; the-hiddenness-of-god.md, "al"; hell-and-the-justice-of-god.md, "am". Sources read word for word in this run, all from GitHub copies: "SEP evil" (Michael Tooley, "The Problem of Evil," first published 2002, revised 2015), "SEP skeptical theism" (2023), "SEP hiddenness" ("Hiddenness of God," 2016, revised 2022), "SEP heaven and hell" ("Heaven and Hell in Christian Thought," 2013, revised 2021), all in `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`; "IEP evidential" (Nick Trakakis, "The Evidential Problem of Evil," Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy, the whole article in `Charley-xiao/Sopher`, `raw_data/https___iep.utm.edu_evil-evi_.txt`); "IEP logical" (James R. Beebe, "Logical Problem of Evil," IEP, as excerpted with its quotations string-checked against the page in `erkkimon/brain-implant-philosophy`, `_implant/raw/iep-evil-log-beebe-and-evil-evi-trakakis-mackie-plantinga-rowe-wykstra.md`); "H-S note" (a study note on GitHub, `hevangel/ai_philosophy`, `knowledge-base/readings-kb/entries/philosophy-of-religion/logical-problem-of-evil.md`, which quotes from Daniel Howard-Snyder's introduction to *The Evidential Argument from Evil* (1996) and from Rowe's essay reprinted there); "Garnett" (Dostoevsky, *The Brothers Karamazov*, translated by Constance Garnett, Project Gutenberg #28054, `GITenberg/The-Brothers-Karamazov_28054`); "Hume" (*Dialogues Concerning Natural Religion*, GITenberg #4583); "Pascal" (*Pensées*, Project Gutenberg #18269, the Dutton edition of 1958 with T. S. Eliot's introduction, in `izak/books`); "ANF Origen" (*De Principiis*, Ante-Nicene Fathers vol. 4, in `jennica/jennica.github.io`); "Confessions" (`pmachapman/Confessions`: the Augsburg Confession, the Westminster Confession, the Athanasian Creed). Mackie, Plantinga, Rowe, Wykstra, Hick, Adams and Schellenberg were not read in their own works; their arguments are paraphrased from the reference works named, and no sentence of theirs is quoted. Scripture is quoted from the site's BSB and checked by script. The earlier rows this area reuses are named where used ("suffering answer" for the rows under "If God is good, why is there so much suffering?", "hell answer" for "Is hell forever, or does it end?", "natural evil answer" for "Why does God allow earthquakes, cancer, and animal suffering?"). No living author is quoted; no consensus is given as a percentage or head-count.

### ai. "Didn't J. L. Mackie show that the two can't both be true?" (the-logical-problem-of-evil)

| Claim | How verified | Result |

# Eighteenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the evil area, and the area "God, faith and reason"

Method words as in the fourteenth to seventeenth runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, found with GitHub's code search (`gh search code`) or by its known path; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches. No WebSearch was run in this run, so nothing below rests on a search summary that was not already in an earlier row. "SEP <title>" means an entry of the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy read in the copy kept on GitHub (`tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/<title>.txt`; each file opens with the entry's first-published and revision dates). "IEP logical" is the excerpt file of James R. Beebe's "Logical Problem of Evil" (Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy) used in the seventeenth run (`erkkimon/brain-implant-philosophy`, `_implant/raw/iep-evil-log-beebe-and-evil-evi-trakakis-mackie-plantinga-rowe-wykstra.md`); "IEP evidential" is Nick Trakakis's article as in that run. "Note", "Repo" and "BSB" as before.

## The editor's read of the five evil pieces and the area page (changed facts and wording)

Read three times: as an atheist philosopher of religion (is Mackie, Rowe, Draper and Schellenberg each stated in a form he would sign; is any reply overclaimed?); as a bereaved parent who arrived from a search (does any sentence read as an explanation of a loss, or as cold; is the care opening right; is a way to help visible near the top and again at the end?); and as a Christian holding each view of hell in turn (eternal conscious punishment, conditional immortality, the hope of restoration). The care openings were found right and left as they are: each piece's first sentence and the area page's first paragraph point to the wisdom pages and Find Help before any argument, and each piece ends its "On the site" list with Find Help. No sentence was found offering a reason for a particular death or illness. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area stay pending.

| Piece | Before | After | Why, and how verified |
|---|---|---|---|
| ai | "and Mackie himself later granted that his charge of contradiction did not stand." (short answer) | "and Mackie himself later granted that the problem of evil does not, after all, show the central doctrines of theism to be inconsistent, though he still asked whether the defence was a real solution." | Flagged (Mackie's 1982 concession). IEP logical, section 8, quotes *The Miracle of Theism* (1982), p. 154: "Since this defense is formally [that is, logically] possible ... we can concede that the problem of evil does not, after all, show that the central doctrines of theism are logically inconsistent with one another. But whether this offers a real solution of the problem is another question." Verified (a reference work quoting the primary; the book itself not reachable). The atheist reader: "did not stand" gave the concession without its reservation; the paraphrase now carries both, as Mackie wrote them |
| ai | Step 3: "Not even an all-powerful being can cause creatures to freely do only what is right" | "On the view of freedom the defence assumes, not even an all-powerful being can ..." | Atheist reader: a compatibilist critic would not sign the step as an unqualified truth; it holds on the libertarian view the defence assumes, which the "Where Christians differ" section already names |
| aj | "The second premise is one most believers accept." | "The second premise is usually treated, on both sides of the debate, as beyond dispute." | No head-counts. IEP evidential (section 2): the theological premise is "usually thought to be indisputable" |
| aj | "Bart Ehrman ... has written that suffering, more than the textual questions he is known for, is what led him to leave it, and his book *God's Problem* (2008) puts the argument in the voice of someone who has looked at the world's suffering for a long time and can no longer believe it is ruled by a loving God." | "Bart Ehrman, a New Testament scholar who left the Christian faith, made the problem of suffering the subject of a book for general readers, *God's Problem* (2008)." | The reason Ehrman gives for leaving, and the book's voice, rested on an earlier search row only; no readable copy was found (GitHub code search for the title returned nothing). Narrowed to the book and its subject. The claim line "The world's suffering makes it impossible to believe that it is ruled by a loving God" (Ehrman) is removed from the piece's claims for the same reason |
| aj | "A man who looks into his garage and sees no car ..." | "An everyday comparison shows the point: a man who looks into his garage ..." | The garage and the flea are the suffering answer's own comparison, not Wykstra's (seventeenth-run row); set in his paragraph without a marker, they read as his |
| ak | Draper's hypothesis of indifference: "the result of good or evil actions by any being other than themselves and their fellow creatures" | "the result of kind or cruel actions by any persons other than human beings" | Flagged (Draper 1989). SEP "The Problem of Evil" (Tooley, 2002, revised 2015), section 3.3, quotes Draper 1989, p. 13: "neither the nature nor the condition of sentient beings on earth is the result of benevolent or malevolent actions performed by nonhuman persons"; bibliography: "Pain and Pleasure: An Evidential Problem for Theists," *Noûs* 23: 331-350. The page's wording widened his "nonhuman persons" to any being, which he would not sign |
| ak | "After the earthquake that destroyed Lisbon on All Saints' Day, 1 November 1755, the Jesuit Gabriel Malagrida published a pamphlet in 1756 blaming the disaster on the city's sins." | "After the earthquake ..., John Wesley, whose hope for the animals this page cites below, asked in his *Serious Thoughts Occasioned by the Late Earthquake at Lisbon* whether God was "now making inquisition for blood," and answered that if so it was "not surprizing" he should begin with that city." | Flagged (Malagrida 1756). The only readable copy found is a bibliographic footnote in a modern Portuguese novel (Mega Ferreira, *Cartas de Casanova: Lisboa 1757*, in `g-libardi/ContaAI`, book00124.txt: "Juizo da verdadeira causa do terremoto ... Lisboa, Oficina de Manoel Soares, 1756"), which gives the title and year but not what the pamphlet says; its content rested on search rows. Cut. Replaced by a primary text read in full: Wesley's tract in *The Works of the Rev. John Wesley*, vol. 10 (Bristol: William Pine), Project Gutenberg #73519, in `Waeil55/bible`, `data/library/en/free-pg73519/3.json`: "Is there indeed a God that judges the world? And is he now making inquisition for blood? If so, it is not surprizing, he should begin there, where so much blood has been poured on the ground like water?" Draper's prior-probability premise and the SEP's "far from clear" (SEP "The Problem of Evil", 3.3: "it is far from clear why the prior probability of this being so should be greater than the prior probability of theism"): matches. Not reached and therefore not used: the text of Malagrida's pamphlet, *Essentials* (1988), the first edition of Talbott's book, and Ehrman's own account in *God's Problem* of why he left the faith.

## The area "god" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/god/` and `client/public/questions/god/`: the area page index.md; the-cosmological-argument.md, "an"; who-made-god.md, "ao"; the-ontological-argument.md, "ap"; the-moral-argument-and-euthyphro.md, "aq"; the-argument-from-reason.md, "ar". The plan's slug (`god`) and title ("God, faith and reason", as in `areas.json`). The plan lists sixteen questions for the area; this run took five, folding plan items 2 (who made God), 3 (a universe from nothing), 4 (the kalam), 5 (contingency and the brute fact), 6 (the moral argument, with the Euthyphro, plan item 9.2) and 13 (the argument from reason) into four pages, and adding the ontological argument, which the plan's list lacked and the brief named.

Primary texts read word for word in this run, all public domain, all from GitHub copies: "Hume" (*Dialogues Concerning Natural Religion*, GITenberg #4583, the copy fetched in the seventeenth run, part 9, lines 2085 to 2175: the speaker who answers the a priori argument is Cleanthes); "Leibniz" (*The Principles of Nature and of Grace*, 1714, translated by Robert Latta, 1898: the text in `BenjaminPoole/ecumenical-christian-library`, `scientists/gottfried-wilhelm-leibniz/principles-of-nature-and-of-grace.html`, and the same text with its front matter naming "translator: Robert Latta", "year: 1898", "written: 1714" in `BenjaminPoole/Ecumenical-Christian-Library-Obsidian-Vault`; sections 7 to 9); "Aquinas" (*Summa Theologica*, part I, translated by the Fathers of the English Dominican Province, Project Gutenberg #17611, the GITenberg copy already in the scratch directory: q. 2, a. 1 and a. 3; q. 3, a. 7; q. 6, a. 3 and a. 4); "Kant" (*The Critique of Pure Reason*, translated by J. M. D. Meiklejohn, GITenberg `The-Critique-of-Pure-Reason_4280`, file 4280-0.txt: "Of the Impossibility of an Ontological Proof", lines 15147 to 15185; "Of the Impossibility of a Cosmological Proof", lines 15251 to 15362); "Deane" (Anselm, *Proslogium*, and Gaunilo, *In Behalf of the Fool*, with Anselm's reply, translated by Sidney Norton Deane, Open Court, 1903: the Proslogium preface and chapters 1 to 5 in `nathanwdavis/auto-zettel-sandbox`, `raw/202609010830-anselm-proslogium-deane-pref-ch1-5.txt`, a capture of the Fordham Medieval Sourcebook; Gaunilo and Anselm's Apologetic in `BenjaminPoole/ecumenical-christian-library`, `medieval/anselm-of-canterbury/behalf-of-the-fool/`, files ibf-01, ibf-03 and ibf-05; the index page dates the Proslogium 1077 to 1078 and Gaunilo about 1078); "Jowett" (Plato, *Euthyphro*, translated by Benjamin Jowett, Project Gutenberg #1642, in `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `dialogues/euthyphro.txt`); "Russell 1927" (the lecture's text in `yogthos/revenant`, `data/corpus/russell/why_i_am_not_a_christian.txt`, read in the seventeenth run and again this run, line 15); "Darwin" (*The Life and Letters of Charles Darwin*, vol. 1, GITenberg `Life-and-Letters-of-Charles-Darwin---Volume-1_2087`, file 2087.txt, lines 9545 to 9584).

Reference works read in full this run in GitHub copies: "SEP <title>" as defined above: "Cosmological Argument" (2004, revised 2022), "Ontological Arguments" (1996, revised 2019), "Moral Arguments for the Existence of God" (2014, revised 2022), "Atheism and Agnosticism", "Divine Simplicity" (2006, revised 2019), "Religion and Science", "Consciousness", "Dualism"; "IEP <title>" (Internet Encyclopedia of Philosophy articles in `Charley-xiao/Sopher`, `raw_data/https___iep.utm.edu_<name>_.txt`, branch master): "Atheism" (atheism), "New Atheists" (n-atheis and new-atheism, two versions of one article), "Hard Problem of Consciousness" (hard-problem-of-conciousness), "Divine Simplicity" (divine-simplicity), "Philosophy of Religion" (religion). "Wikipedia copy" as before: a tertiary source, used only for a plain fact and marked so. Scripture is quoted from the site's BSB and checked by script; the creeds from the site's own texts (`client/public/creeds/documents/`). Living authors (Craig, Plantinga, Swinburne, Oppy, Pruss, van Inwagen, Wielenberg, Street, Dennett, Chalmers, Nagel, Krauss, Dawkins, Sober, Zimmerman, Beilby) are paraphrased from the reference entries named, never quoted. No consensus is given as a percentage or head-count; where a reference work's own judgment is reported ("none has a large following", "no single metaethical theory seems to enjoy widespread support"), it is attributed to that work.

Agreement: the God answer gives the contingency argument in two premises and puts the weight on the first, with Russell; this page lays it out in three, puts the weight on the same premise and names which premise each objection denies. It agrees with the science piece on the Big Bang, Borde-Guth-Vilenkin and the quantum "nothing", and takes no position on the age of the universe. The Note on Exodus 3 (aseity) and the Note on Acts 17 (Paul's speech) agree.

Trimmed before the area was finished, to bring the argued prose from 2,462 to 2,361 words: Hawking and Mlodinow's book (the science piece names it), Craig's "philosophical arguments against an infinite past", the sentence on personal explanation, and some repetition in the objection and the evidence. Cut or not used: Taylor's sentence on the principle as "a presupposition of reason itself" (quoted in the SEP; paraphrased instead); Gale and Pruss's weak principle and Oppy's reply (too technical for the page); Craig's library of infinitely many books (the argument is described, not illustrated); Smith's and Grünbaum's names (the arguments are given without them).

### ao. "If everything needs a cause, who made God?" (who-made-god)

If there can be anything without a cause, it may just as well be the world as God." | Russell 1927 (GH), line 15, read this run; hell-answer row (date and place) | Quoted. Agreement: the God answer answers Dawkins in one paragraph (an explanation needn't be explained first; God is not a complicated machine; he is the necessary being); this page agrees and goes further, with Aquinas's reason for simplicity and the dispute over it among Christians. The Explorer film "Who made God?" (`client/public/explorer/films/who-made-god.json`, read this run) says the question is "a fair question about the wrong kind of answer" and that God "is not the first link in the chain"; this page says the same. There is no Honest Answer titled "Who made God?" in `client/src/data/answers.ts`; the brief's reference was to that film and to the paragraph in the God answer.

The Athanasian Creed in the site's text reads "nor three uncreated, but one uncreated"; the page's first draft quoted it as "not three uncreated", and before commit the quotation was changed to begin at "three uncreated", so that the creed's word is not altered. Final wording: the Creed confesses "The Father uncreate, the Son uncreate, and the Holy Ghost uncreate," and that there are not "three uncreated, but one uncreated."

Cut or not used: Mill's own text (not read; given only as Russell quotes it); Wielenberg's 2009 article on Dawkins (title seen in the SEP bibliography, content not read); any claim that "many" apologists state the argument badly (the page says "sometimes").

### ap. "Can anyone prove that God exists just by thinking about the idea of God?" (the-ontological-argument)

| Claim | How verified | Result |
|---|---|---|

# Nineteenth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the god area, and the area "Morality, sexuality and the church's record"

Method words as in the fourteenth to eighteenth runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, found with GitHub's code search (`gh search code`) and fetched from raw.githubusercontent.com; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches. No WebSearch was run in this run, so nothing below rests on a search summary. "SEP <title>" and "IEP <title>" are the reference entries read in the GitHub copies named in the eighteenth run (kept in the scratch directory as fetched then). "Note", "Repo" and "BSB" as before.

## The editor's read of the five god pieces and the area page (changed facts and wording)

Read four times: as a working atheist philosopher (is each objection put in a form Hume, Kant, Russell, Mackie, Oppy, Wielenberg, Street or Dawkins would sign; is any premise smuggled; is any reply overclaimed; is each numbered argument valid as stated?); as a physicist (is anything about cosmology or quantum theory stated beyond what is known?); as a Thomist (is Aquinas given fairly, the third way, simplicity, the "self-evident in itself, not to us" distinction, natural law?); and as a Reformed epistemologist (is the claim that belief in God can be warranted without argument stated as its holders state it?). Every numbered argument was checked for validity: two were not valid as stated and are mended (the cosmological argument's second step; the argument from reason's second step), and one smuggled a premise (the moral argument's first step). The sources the eighteenth run held only through reference entries or tertiary copies were rechecked one by one: each was verified in a readable copy, or its paraphrase is now marked as the named reference entry's account, or it was cut. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). Four of the flagged sources sit in the evil area's pieces (Mackie 1982 in the logical piece, Marilyn Adams in the evidential and hell pieces, Lewis in the natural-evil and hell pieces, Aquinas I-II q.87 in the hell piece); they were rechecked and mended there too. All ten pieces and both areas stay pending.

| Piece | Before | After | Why, and how verified |
|---|---|---|---|
| an | 2. The universe, all of space, time, matter and energy, together with the physical laws and states from which they arise, is contingent: | 2. The universe, all of space, time, matter and energy, together with the physical laws and states from which they arise and anything else that might not have existed, is contingent: | Atheist logician: step 3 says every contingent thing is part of what is being explained, but step 2 defined the universe as physical only, so a contingent non-physical thing could explain it and the conclusion did not follow. Checked and left. Leibniz's translator: besides the front matter of the eighteenth run's copy, a second, independent copy on GitHub (`zriyansh/the-god-question`, `library/philosophy/leibniz-monadology-principles/text.txt`) heads both texts "by Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, translated by Robert Latta (1898)", and its sections 7 to 9 match every phrase the cosmological and who-made-God pages quote ("nothing takes place without sufficient reason"; "Why does something exist rather than nothing? For 'nothing' is simpler and easier than 'something.'"; "the sequence of contingent things"; "the same question always remains"; "a necessary being, bearing in itself the reason of its own existence"; "power, knowledge and will in perfection"). Verified (two copies naming the translator; no title page read). Hume's *Dialogues* part 9, Kant in Meiklejohn, Anselm and Gaunilo in Deane, Aquinas I q.2 a.1 and a.3, q.3 a.7, q.6 a.3, Plato in Jowett, Russell's lecture of 1927 and Darwin's letter of 3 July 1881: rechecked against the eighteenth run's copies in the scratch directory; every quotation matches. Robert Adams (moral piece): no readable copy of *Finite and Infinite Goods*; the summary was already attributed to SEP "Moral Arguments" and the marker now covers the whole summary. Plantinga in the ontological piece: already attributed to SEP "Ontological Arguments" ("as the same encyclopedia reports"); left. Plantinga's *Does God Have a Nature?* (1980): IEP "Divine Simplicity" (GH, r18) sets out his objection that simplicity is "flatly incompatible with the obvious fact that God has several properties" and that "God is obviously a person"; the page's sentence matches. Russell's remark on ontological arguments, Findlay 1948, Street 2006 and Mackie 1977: as the eighteenth run's rows give them, from the entries named there. No living author is quoted in the ten pieces; no consensus is given as a percentage or head-count ("many philosophers" in the moral piece is the SEP's own judgment, attributed to it). Every BSB quotation in the ten pieces was rechecked by script after the changes (none was added or changed).

Physics. Besides the Borde, Guth and Vilenkin sentence, the cosmological page was read for any claim beyond what is known: Hawking's no-boundary proposal (described, not endorsed), quantum events that "seem to happen without any sufficient cause" (as on the standard interpretations; the page names the deterministic ones), Albert's objection to the quantum "nothing", and no claim that the Big Bang proves God. Nothing else changed.

Not reached and therefore not used as primary: Mackie's *The Miracle of Theism*, Wielenberg's two books, Robert Adams's *Finite and Infinite Goods*, Marilyn Adams's essay and book, Plantinga's books, Flew's article, the Fitelson and Sober article, and the second chapter of Lewis's *The Problem of Pain*. Each is now either marked as a named reference entry's account or cut.

## The area "morality" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/morality/` and `client/public/questions/morality/`: the area page index.md; the-crusades.md, "as"; the-inquisition.md, "at"; slavery-and-conquest.md, "au"; abuse-and-cover-up.md, "av"; does-religion-poison-everything.md, "aw". The plan's slug (`morality`) and title ("Morality, sexuality and the church's record", as in `areas.json`). The plan's build order (section 4.5) puts area 9 after science and before areas 8 and 10; area 8 is written, so area 9 was the plan's next. Of its fourteen questions this run took the church's record: plan items 4 (the Crusades), 5 (the Inquisition and the burning of heretics), 3 and 7 together (slavery and colonialism), 8 (abuse and its cover-up) and 12 (Hitchens, "religion poisons everything", with religious violence compared honestly). The pages on morality and sexuality are not written; the area page says so.

Sources, and the words used for them below. Primary texts read word for word in this run, all from GitHub copies fetched from raw.githubusercontent.com: "Hume HE" (David Hume, *The History of England*, vol. 1, chapter 5, the Hume Texts Online edition in `earlytexts/englishphilosophy`, `texts/data/hume/he/1/5.mit`, whose copytext is the edition of 1778; its index files give vol. 1 as published in 1762); "Paine" (*The Age of Reason*, part 2, preface dated "October, 1795", the fifteenth run's copy from `dbactual/decl-of-ind`, kept in the scratch directory); "Augustine" (*A Treatise Concerning the Correction of the Donatists*, Letter 185, NPNF, in `HistoricalChristianFaith/Writings-Database`, whose note dates it "c. 417"); "Aquinas" (*Summa Theologiae* II-II q.11 a.3 and I-II q.87 a.4, English Dominican translation, Benziger 1947, in `GapEye/summa-theologica-dataset`); "Tertullian" (*To Scapula*, ANF vol. 3, translated by S. Thelwall, in `scrollmapper/bible_databases_deuterocanonical` and in the CCEL capture in `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `fathers/schaff/ANF-03/anf03-12.htm`); "Lactantius" (*Divine Institutes* book 5, ANF vol. 7, the same two repositories, `anf07-08.htm`); "Douglass" (*Narrative of the Life of Frederick Douglass* (1845), with its appendix, in `ncteisen/the-hypertextual-library`, `books/frederick_douglass/text.txt`); "Las Casas" (*A Brief Account of the Destruction of the Indies*, the English translation printed in London in 1689, Project Gutenberg #20321, in `catekkesin/507homework1`). News and legal texts read word for word: "NYT 2004" (*The New York Times*, the front-page report of 27 February 2004 on the two studies commissioned by the American bishops, with its correction of 5 March 2004, in `nirmal91/PennAppsSpring2014`, `resources/nyt_docs/02/27/1562180.txt`); "NYT 2022" (*The New York Times*, 23 and 24 May 2022, headline, summary and opening sentences, in `wckoeppen/headliner`, `the-new-york-times-2022-05-24.csv`); "PA 2006" and "TN 2008" (court opinions that cite the 2004 reports by their titles: `lexifina/united-states-pennsylvania-other-courts-caselaw`, Lackawanna County, 2006, 79 Pa. D. & C. 4th 464; `lexifina/united-states-tennessee-appellate-caselaw`, 306 S.W.3d 712, 2008). Reference and tertiary: "IEP New Atheists" (the eighteenth run's copy); "WP <title>" (an English Wikipedia article read in a copy on GitHub, a tertiary source used only for plain facts of date and event and for estimates it attributes to a named historian, and marked so: "Crusades" in `NikilMunireddy/Document-Indexing`; "Spanish Inquisition" in `adamrpah/CSSMA` (the 2007 Schools Wikipedia text); "First Crusade", "Fourth Crusade", "John Calvin", "Thirty Years' War" in `jeffreywpli/convex_case`, a copy whose sentences are shuffled, so only facts stated within one sentence are used from it; "Slavery", "Abolitionism", "Colonialism" in `cabosanlucas/document_similarity_using_tfidf`; "Huguenot" in `NikilMunireddy/Document-Indexing`; the infobox of "Michael Servetus" in `chapmanjacobd/wiki-welcome`). "Note" is the Study Bible's note in the repository. "Answer row" is a row already verified in this ledger for the Honest Answer "Why didn't the Bible just ban slavery?" or in the essay ledgers under `docs/work/essays/ledgers/`, used only where marked and not rechecked against a primary in this run.

## The editor's read of the five morality pieces and the area page (changed facts and wording)

Read four times, as the brief asked: as a survivor of abuse in a church (is anything cold, defensive or self-protective; does any sentence ask the reader to trust, return, forgive or keep quiet; are Find Help and the police at the start and at the end; is anything about the law on reporting given as legal advice?); as a Jewish reader and a Muslim reader of the Crusades and Inquisition pages (is every massacre named without softening; is anything said of Judaism or Islam that its adherents would not sign; is comparative history used to shrink guilt?); as a descendant of enslaved people reading the slavery page; and as a historian checking every date, name and figure. Every fact the nineteenth run held only from a Wikipedia copy (tertiary) was looked for in a second, independent readable source: confirmed facts stay, with the source named below; the rest are softened to an undated or unnumbered statement or cut. The two sets of abuse figures, Kamen's estimate and Hillenbrand's year were treated as the brief directed. No sentence says or implies which church body the author or any reader belongs to; church bodies are named only as history, in the third person. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area stay pending.

| Piece | Before | After | Why, and how verified |
|---|---|---|---|

The charge in the question is true. Church leaders, Catholic and Protestant alike, leaders of the Southern Baptist Convention among them, knew of abuse | Survivor reader: the first lines now say what to do if a child is in danger now, and name the crime precisely ("abuse" alone could be heard as every kind of harm, not all of which the police can act on). | as | The Crusades were a holy war, preached by popes and fought under the cross, and the worst of what was done in them was done in Christ's name: the Jewish communities of Worms and Mainz destroyed in 1096, the killing in Jerusalem in July 1099, a Christian city sacked in 1204. | au | Then look at who first resisted. In the Spanish colonies Dominican friars at Santo Domingo denounced the enslavement of the native peoples as unjust and illegal, and Las Casas, who had come to the Indies as a settler and was present at a massacre in Cuba, turned against the enslavement he had seen. He first proposed bringing African slaves in the native peoples' place, and later changed his mind and became an advocate for the Africans too. His account of the destruction of the Indies helped bring about the New Laws of 1542, which abolished the enslavement of native peoples for the first time in European colonial history, and in 1537 Pope Paul III forbade the enslavement of the peoples of the Americas. Later the British campaign against the trade was led in Parliament by William Wilberforce, an evangelical, after the committee against the trade was formed in 1787; the trade was outlawed throughout the British Empire on 25 March 1807, and slavery itself by the act of 1833. | Then look at who resisted. The enslaved resisted first, and longest. Within the church, in the Spanish colonies, the Dominican friars of Santo Domingo, Antonio de Montesinos first among them, preached against the settlers' cruelty to the native peoples, and Las Casas, who had himself held native laborers in an *encomienda* and was present at a massacre in Cuba, gave them up and turned against the system he had served. He first supported bringing African slaves in the native peoples' place, and later wrote of himself in his *History of the Indies* that "afterwards, not for everything in the world would he have offered it, for he always held that they were made slaves by injustice and tyranny, the same reasoning applying to them as to the Indians" (in MacNutt's translation, 1909). In 1537 Pope Paul III, in the bull *Sublimis Deus*, declared that the peoples of the Americas were not to be deprived of their liberty, and Las Casas's campaign helped bring about the New Laws of 1542. Later the British campaign against the trade was led in Parliament by William Wilberforce, an evangelical, after a committee for the abolition of the trade was formed in 1787; Parliament abolished the British slave trade in 1807, and slavery in the British colonies by the act of 1833. | Flagged (tertiary) and descendant reader. Confirmed in second sources: MacNutt, *Bartholomew de Las Casas; his life, apostolate, and writings* (Cleveland: Arthur H. Clark, 1909), GITenberg #23466: ch. 5, "The sermons of Fray Antonio de Montesinos" and the Dominicans' protest at "the injustice and cruelty daily practised on the natives"; Las Casas's "own Indians" and his renouncing his "encomienda"; the massacre at Caonao in Cuba; his assent to importing Africans and the passage of his *Historia* (vol. 4, p. 380, as MacNutt cites it) now quoted; the New Laws "drawn up" in 1542 under his influence; the bull *Sublimis Deus*, printed in MacNutt's appendix II, "Given in Rome in the year of our Lord 1537". Thomas Clarkson's *History of ... the Abolition of the African Slave-Trade* (1808; 1839 ed.), GITenberg #10633: the committee of May 1787; "Bill for the total abolition carried" in 1807; the act of 1833. Cut as unconfirmed: "for the first time in European colonial history", "25 March", "throughout the British Empire", "denounced ... as unjust and illegal", and that Las Casas "became an advocate for the Africans" (he wrote of his error; the stronger phrase was not in the source). Descendant reader: the enslaved are now named as the first to resist, before any church's witness |
| au | The enslaved heard Exodus read by the men who owned them, "I have indeed seen the affliction of My people in Egypt" (Exodus 3:7, BSB), and they knew whose story it was. | Exodus was read to the enslaved by the men who owned them, and it tells of a God who says, "I have indeed seen the affliction of My people in Egypt" (Exodus 3:7, BSB). Whose story that is was never hard to see. | Descendant reader and historian: "they knew whose story it was" spoke for the inner life of millions of people from no source; the sentence now makes the page's own theological claim about the text, not a claim about what the enslaved thought |
| aw | crusades, the stake, wars between Catholics and Protestants that emptied whole regions of Europe. | crusades, the stake, wars between Catholics and Protestants that laid waste whole regions of Europe. | Historian: "emptied" leaned on a population figure that is cut below |

In 1685 Louis XIV abolished the legal recognition of Protestantism in France and forced the Huguenots to convert. The Thirty Years' War, which began in 1618 and ended with the Peace of Westphalia in 1648, devastated central Europe; estimates vary by region, but the population of the German states is generally thought to have fallen by a quarter to two fifths, and by more in some places, from war, famine and disease. | In France Catholics and Protestants fought a series of civil wars between 1562 and 1598, and in the St. Bartholomew's Day Massacre, which began in Paris on 24 August 1572, Catholics massacred Huguenots in the capital and then in Lyons, Toulouse, Bordeaux, Rouen and other towns; how many were killed is not known. In 1685 Louis XIV revoked the Edict of Nantes, which had given the Huguenots legal toleration, after years in which soldiers had been quartered on Protestant families to force them to convert. The Thirty Years' War, which began in 1618 and ended with the Peace of Westphalia in 1648, devastated central Europe through war, famine and disease; estimates of how many died differ widely from region to region, and this page gives no figure. | Flagged (tertiary). Confirmed in a second source, the Catholic Encyclopedia (1907 to 1912), read in the copy in gustavo-depaula/ember (content/books/catholic-encyclopedia/en-US/): "Huguenots" (the militant period 1559-98; January and March 1562; the Edict of Nantes 1598; "revoked the Edict of Nantes on 18 October, 1685"; the *dragonnade*, "making converts by force"); "Saint Bartholomew's Day" ("occurred in Paris on 24 August, 1572"; "In the provinces also massacres occurred"; "Lyons, Toulouse, Bordeaux, and Rouen all had their massacres"; "The number of victims is unknown"); "The Thirty Years War" ("(1618-48)"; "the Peace of Westphalia with France and Sweden was settled on 24 October, 1648"). An. Dom. 1542"); the Thirty Years' War, 1618 to 1648, and the Peace of Westphalia (CE). The Peace of Augsburg's settlement and the quarrels over it (CE "The Thirty Years War": changes "contrary to the Religious Peace of Augsburg"). The Southern Baptist Convention's founding in 1845 and resolution of 1995 were already in two sources (the tradition study quoting sbc.net, and the Honest Answer's row); kept, in the third person, with the body named exactly.

Care, as read this run. The abuse page: Find Help and the police are in its first sentence and its last paragraph; it tells no one to trust, return or forgive; it states no law and now says it is not legal advice; it names no act. The Crusades and Inquisition pages: each massacre named (Worms and Mainz; Jerusalem's Muslims and the Jews burned in their synagogue; Constantinople; the burnings of *conversos*; the expulsion of 1492); nothing is said of Islam or Judaism beyond the history; the one comparative fact (the Turkish conquests before 1095) corrects a false claim and says it lessens nothing. The slavery page: the enslaved are named as the first to resist; Douglass is named in the short answer; no claim is made about what the enslaved thought.

Not reached, and therefore cut or not used: Kamen's book; any second source for the 10,667 figure, for the 2022 report's length or the list's size; Hillenbrand's book and the Riley-Smiths'; Franz's population estimate; John Paul II's statements on 1204; Innocent III's prohibition before Zara; the day of the expulsion edict (Lea gives 30 March, the Wikipedia copy 31 March).

## The area "religions" (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/religions/` and `client/public/questions/religions/`: the area page index.md; are-all-religions-the-same.md, "ax"; is-it-arrogant-to-say-jesus-is-the-only-way.md, "ay"; those-who-never-heard.md, "az"; judaism-islam-and-jesus.md, "ba"; only-a-christian-because-of-where-you-were-born.md, "bb". The plan's slug (`religions`) and title ("Other religions, and those who never heard", as in `areas.json`). Of the plan's ten questions this run took items 7 with 1 (all religions the same; the only way), 1 again as the charge of arrogance, 5 (those who never heard), 2 with 3 (Islam, and Judaism and Jesus, in one page), and 6 (where you were born).

Sources, and the words used for them below. Reference work read in full this run: "SEP Diversity" (the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, "Religious Diversity (Pluralism)", first published 2004, revised 2020, in `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/Religious Diversity Pluralism.txt`), which quotes Hick (1980, 1983, 1984, 1997a), Smith (1976), Runzo (1988) and Plantinga (1997, 2000) and summarizes Rahner, Himma, Quinn, Heim and Mutahhari; "SEP Ultimates" ("God and Other Ultimates", 2021, same repository). Primary texts read word for word, all public domain, all GH: "Mill" (John Stuart Mill, *On Liberty*, GITenberg #34901; its introduction: "_Liberty_ was published in 1859"); "Schechter" (Solomon Schechter, *Studies in Judaism*, first series, GITenberg #48890, introduction dated "CAMBRIDGE, _February 1896_", essay "The Dogmas of Judaism"); "Abrahams" (Israel Abrahams, *Judaism*, GITenberg #6971, title page "Reader in Talmudic and Rabbinic Literature, University of Cambridge", foreword "the writer is himself a Jew"; no year is printed in the copy, so none is given and the book is not in a reading list); "Pickthall" (the Qur'an in Mohammed Marmaduke Pickthall's translation, the Tanzil text in `Q-Translate/translations`, `en.pickthall.txt`, whose footer names the translator; title and year, *The Meaning of the Glorious Koran* (1930), from three independent GitHub project notes: `SafhaJournal/pickthall-modernized` and `embabel-worlds/realm-quran` READMEs, `co-codin/Bible-RAG` docs/DECISIONS.md; public domain in the United States since 1 January 2026; checked against Rodwell's translation, GITenberg #2800 and #3434, for sense only); "CE Islam" (*Catholic Encyclopedia*, "Mohammed and Mohammedanism", 10424a, used only for the five pillars). "Note" is the Study Bible's note in the repository; "Answer row" a row already verified in this ledger for an Honest Answer, reused and marked, not rechecked against a primary this run. Scripture is quoted from the BSB and checked by script. No living author is quoted: Hick (who died in 2012) and Wilfred Cantwell Smith (who died in 2000) are quoted only as the SEP quotes them, and neither death date was confirmed in a readable source this run; Himma, Runzo, Plantinga, Heim, Quinn's heirs, Bauckham, Boyarin and Hurtado are paraphrased from named reference entries or notes. No consensus is given as a percentage or head-count.

# Twenty-first run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the religions area, and five more pieces in the area "The historical Jesus and the resurrection"

Method words as in the fourteenth to twentieth runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, found with GitHub's code or repository search and fetched from raw.githubusercontent.com; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches. No WebSearch was run, so nothing below rests on a search summary. "Note" is the Study Bible's note in the repository (`client/public/bible/notes/`), read this run in full for every chapter named. New sources read this run, all GH: "CE Apocatastasis" and "CE Origen" (the *Catholic Encyclopedia*, 1907 to 1912, "Apocatastasis" 01599a and "Origen and Origenism" 11306b, in `gustavo-depaula/ember`); "SEP PoR" and "SEP Mysticism" (the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, "Philosophy of Religion", first published 2007, revised 2023, and "Mysticism", the twentieth run's copies from `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, now read for Hick); "SEP Arabic" ("Arabic and Islamic Philosophy of Religion", same repository); "ANF 1" (the *Ante-Nicene Fathers*, vol. 1, the CCEL capture in `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `fathers/schaff/ANF-01/`: 1 Clement, `anf01-05.htm`; Ignatius to the Ephesians and the Smyrnaeans, `anf01-16.htm` and `anf01-21.htm`; Justin's *First Apology*, `anf01-46.htm`; Justin's *Dialogue with Trypho*, `anf01-48.htm`); "Eusebius" (*Church History*, McGiffert's translation, NPNF second series vol. 1, the same capture, `fathers/schaff/NPNF2-01/Npnf2-01-07.htm` and `-08.htm`, books 2 and 3); "Crombie" (Origen, *Against Celsus*, Frederick Crombie's translation, *The Writings of Origen*, vol. 1, Gutenberg #70561, the copy in the scratch directory since the fifteenth run); "Whiston" (Josephus, *Antiquities of the Jews*, William Whiston's translation, GITenberg `Antiquities-of-the-Jews_2848`); "Gibbon 16" (Edward Gibbon, *The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire*, chapter 16 with its notes, GITenberg `History-of-the-Decline-and-Fall-of-the-Roman-Empire---Volume-2_732`; the date of the first volume, 1776, from the preface dated "Bentinck Street, February 1, 1776" and the editor's note "This volume was published in 1776" in GITenberg `..._Volume-1_731`); "Schweitzer" (Albert Schweitzer, *The Quest of the Historical Jesus*, translated by W. Montgomery, second English edition, London: A. and C. Black, 1911, whose verso says "First Edition published March 1910", GITenberg `The-Quest-of-the-Historical-Jesus_45422`); "Russell" (Bertrand Russell, *Why I Am Not a Christian*, the lecture of 1927, the seventeenth run's copy); "Lewis WLN" (C. S. Lewis, "The World's Last Night", in `HistoricalChristianFaith/Commentaries-Database`, `CS Lewis/Mark 13_30-32.toml`, a transcript whose source is given as a page at singjupost.com; one copy only, so only the phrase two independent pages share is quoted); "WP" (English Wikipedia articles in GitHub copies, tertiary, used only where marked: "Adoptionism", "Christology" and "Resurrection of Jesus" in `kirito-0512/data`, `dump/`, which print no reference lists).

## The editor's read of the five religions pieces and the area page (changed facts and wording)

Read five times, as the brief asked: as a rabbi (is Judaism described as its adherents would sign; is anything said of the Talmud, the Messiah or the law a Jewish reader would call a Christian caricature; is rabbinic Judaism treated as a living faith and not a stage on the way to Christianity; are the church's wrongs named without softening?); as an imam (the same for Islam; is every Qur'an verse quoted exactly from Pickthall, with its reference and in context; is the sentence that adherents read these texts within their own traditions present; is nothing said of any modern state or conflict?); as a pluralist philosopher (is Hick stated at full strength; is the reply to the elephant parable fair or a score?); as a grieving reader of the page on those who never heard (is the care paragraph near the top as well as at the end; does any sentence guess at a person's fate?); and as a historian and logician checking every date, name, quotation and inference. Every fact the twentieth run marked as resting on an answer row or as not verified was looked for in a readable source: confirmed facts stay with the source named; the rest are softened or cut. The 553 question was settled as the brief directed. Hick (died 2012) and Wilfred Cantwell Smith (died 2000) are quoted only as the Stanford Encyclopedia quotes them; their death dates are the brief's and were not read in a source here, and the pages give none. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). The hell page in the evil area carried the same sentence about 553 that the page on those who never heard follows; it was mended in the same words so that the two pages still agree. All five pieces, the area and the hell page stay pending.

It tells the Muslim and the Christian, who disagree about whether a man died on a cross, that their disagreement doesn't decide the way to the Real. Checked and left. Every Qur'an verse on the five pages (112:3, 4:171, 4:157, 4:158, 33:40, 29:46, 3:45, 3:95) was compared word for word with Pickthall's text in the twentieth run's copy; each page that quotes the Qur'an says that its adherents read it within their own tradition; no verse with an exclamation is quoted. Schechter's three sentences, Abrahams's phrases and Greenstone's words as Abrahams quotes them, Mill's three sentences, Smith's sentence, the Hick quotations in the Stanford Encyclopedia's article on religious diversity and the Nicene Creed's clause were rechecked against the copies by script (all match). The five pillars: CE "Mohammed and Mohammedanism" (10424a), read again this run: "the recital of the Creed and prayers, fasting, almsgiving, and the pilgrimage to Mecca"; "The daily prayers are five in number"; fasting "prescribed only in the month of Ramadan". The Note on Matthew 27 for the verse's history and *Nostra Aetate*; the Note on John 16 for 1096 and 1543; the Note on Romans 11 for "That reading is false, and this site rejects it" and the views of Israel's place; the Note on Mark 8 for the Jewish objection about the Messiah's work; the Note on John 14 for *Lumen Gentium*, Pinnock, Nash, Newbigin and Rahner's name. The middle-knowledge sentence matches the Honest Answer on those who never heard ("God, who knows what every person would freely do in any circumstance, may have so ordered the world that those who never hear are those who would not have believed if they had") and stays attributed to it. Every BSB quotation was rechecked by script after the changes (three with an ellipsis checked by hand, none changed).

Care, as read this run. The page on those who never heard has the care sentence first in its short answer and the care paragraph at its end; no sentence names, hints at or guesses any person's fate; universal restoration is stated without contempt and now as historians can support it. The Judaism and Islam page names the church's wrongs against Jews down to the Shoah and the 1994 repudiation of Luther's writings, says rabbinic Judaism is a living faith in its own right, quotes 4:171 and 4:158 so that Islam's honour to Jesus stands beside its denial, takes no side on Israel's place or on whether Christians and Muslims worship the same God, and says nothing of any modern state or conflict.

Not reached, and therefore cut or not used as primary: Lawson on the readings of 4:157; any source for Loftus's biography; Newbigin's own use of the elephant parable; the content of Hick's 1997 reply beyond its title; the Sanders, Fackre and Nash volume.

## The area "jesus": five more pieces (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/jesus/` and `client/public/questions/jesus/`: how-did-jesus-become-god.md, "bc"; did-jesus-claim-to-be-god.md, "bd"; the-virgin-birth.md, "be"; did-jesus-predict-the-end-within-a-generation.md, "bf"; did-the-disciples-die-for-their-belief.md, "bg". From section 19's unwritten list for the area (the plan's items 5.5, 5.6, 5.11, 5.12 and 5.13). The area page lists them and gains two words.

Living scholars are paraphrased only from the Study Bible's notes (named, with the chapter) or, where marked, from a Wikipedia copy (tertiary), never quoted; Ehrman's books were not reached, and his case is given in the words the notes give it. Scripture is quoted from the BSB and checked by script; quotations with an ellipsis, and the Psalms, by hand. No consensus is given as a percentage or head-count.

Agreement and a finding: the Honest Answers "What if Christianity is wrong?" and "Did the resurrection actually happen?" use the same four deaths and the same limit ("People die for false beliefs all the time"; "men don't die for a story they know they made up"; the evidence for most others "is later and less certain"); this page agrees and links both. One sentence in "What if Christianity is wrong?" is imprecise and is reported, not changed: "the men who had run away were saying they had seen him alive, and some of them were killed for it", followed at once by James the Lord's brother, who was not among the men who ran away (John 7:5 has him an unbeliever during the ministry).
