# Twenty-second run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the five newest jesus pieces, and five more pieces in the area "Who wrote the Gospels, when, and who chose the books"

Method words as in the fourteenth to twenty-first runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, found with GitHub's code search (`gh search code`, which allows about ten queries a minute; a burst returns empty lists, so this run paced its queries) and fetched from raw.githubusercontent.com; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches (the Ehrman blog, Hurtado's blog, the Internet Archive, the Stanford Encyclopedia's own site, Project Gutenberg and CCEL were each tried and did not answer). No WebSearch was run, so nothing below rests on a search summary. "Note" is the Study Bible's note in the repository, dumped this run and read for every chapter named. Sources new to this run, all GH:

- "BT-HJBG": Ehrman's own text from *How Jesus Became God* (2014), the section "Did Jesus Claim to Be God?" at the end of the chapter the reader names as "Did Jesus Think He Was God?", read aloud word for word in a Blogging Theology recording of 26 January 2021, whose timestamped transcript is in `IslamicRevival/IslamicRevival.github.io`, `docs/blogging_theology/Did_Jesus_Claim_to_be_God.md`. Near-primary: the book's words, through a transcript. The reader's own asides (among them a percentage of scholars) are his and are not used. Paraphrased, never quoted, since a transcript can mishear.
- "BT-NOPE": Ehrman's blog post "Nope, Jesus Is Not Yahweh", which the reader dates to 17 April 2021, read aloud in full, with the reader's comments marked off ("stepping aside from bart here"), in a recording of 19 April 2021, transcript in the same repository, `docs/blogging_theology/_Nope_Jesus_is_Not_Yahweh__Dr_Bart_Ehrman.md`. The post itself could not be opened. Paraphrased.
- "BT-INT": an interview with Ehrman, Blogging Theology, 19 December 2021, `docs/blogging_theology/@bartdehrman_discusses_Jesus,_the_Bible,_and_Christianity.md`, an automatic YouTube transcript with its errors ("bart dehrman", "typical rehab"); used only for statements whose sense is unmistakable, and paraphrased, never quoted. The page says "as an automatic transcript of it records".
- "LEWIS": C. S. Lewis, "The World's Last Night", the transcript the twenty-first run found (`HistoricalChristianFaith/Commentaries-Database`, `CS Lewis/Mark 13_30-32.toml`, `source_title="The World's Last Night (Essay)"`), opened and read in full this run.
- "SEP Trinity": the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, "Trinity" (first published 2009, revised 2020), `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/Trinity.txt`; it cites Hurtado (2003; 2005) in its bibliography only, so it adds nothing on his argument.

## The editor's read of the five jesus pieces (changed facts and wording)

Read five times, as the brief asked: as Bart Ehrman (is his exaltation case at full strength, the angel point included, and is the reply an answer or a dodge?); as a Jewish reader of the trial and of Isaiah 7; as a classicist checking every ancient reference and translation; as a logician asking what each argument establishes; and as a believer who was told the disciples all died as martyrs. Every quotation on the five pages that is not Scripture was checked by script against the copies (all match); every BSB quotation by script, four with an ellipsis or two verses by hand. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area page stay pending.

| bc | The language of Nicaea, "of one substance with the Father," is not in the New Testament, and some early texts speak of Jesus being declared Son of God at his resurrection. Bart Ehrman builds on those texts a careful case that the first believers thought God had exalted a man, and that belief in a Son who existed before creation came later. But the earliest writings we can date, Paul's letters from the 50s, already hold both together: the Son God sent, through whom all things came, and the crucified man God exalted. In the same letters Jesus is prayed to in Aramaic, called on as the Lord of Joel's promise and given the words Isaiah kept for God alone, by Jews who confessed the Shema. The high view of Jesus was there at the start. Whether it is true depends on who he was and on what happened at Easter. | Nicaea's "of one substance with the Father" is not in the New Testament, and some early texts speak of Jesus being declared Son of God at his resurrection. Bart Ehrman builds on them a careful case that the first believers thought God had exalted a man, and he grants that within about twenty years Paul wrote of a Christ who existed before his birth, though, on his reading, as a divine being below God. So the question is what that early faith was. In Paul's letters from the 50s Jesus is prayed to in Aramaic, called on as the Lord of Joel's promise, given a part in creation and the words Isaiah kept for God alone, by Jews who confessed the Shema. Worship of that kind was there almost from the start. Whether it was right depends on who he was and on what happened at Easter. | Ehrman reader: the short answer said the first believers' exaltation view preceded belief in pre-existence and replied that Paul 'already' held both, as though that refuted Ehrman. BT-INT (Ehrman's 2021 interview, automatic transcript, paraphrased only) shows that Ehrman himself grants that Paul's Christ in Philippians 2 is pre-existent ('a pre-existent divine being who comes down ... comes to earth as a human and then gets exalted'), though not God 'metaphysically'; the short answer now states that, and claims only what the argument establishes (early worship), not 'the high view was there at the start' |
| bc | The New Testament scholar Bart Ehrman set out the case in *How Jesus Became God* (2014). As the Study Bible's note on Mark 1 summarizes his argument, early Christians first thought Jesus was exalted to divine sonship at his resurrection, then pushed the moment back to his baptism, as Mark does, and later to his birth and to a time before creation. John's Gospel, on this account, with its Word who "was God" (John 1:1, BSB), is the end of a development, not a record of what Jesus or his first followers believed, as the note on John 1 reports Ehrman's view. | Bart Ehrman set out the case in *How Jesus Became God* (2014). As the Study Bible's note on Mark 1 summarizes the book, early Christians first thought Jesus was exalted to divine sonship at his resurrection, then pushed the moment back to his baptism, as Mark does, and later to his birth and to a time before creation. In a 2021 interview, as an automatic transcript of it records, he said that Mark's Jesus is a man who was exalted and became a divine being, with no life before his birth, and that Paul, writing earlier, does speak of a Christ who existed before he became human, though not as God in the later, metaphysical sense. On his blog that year, in a post read aloud in full in a public recording, he argued that the name above every name gives Jesus, at his exaltation and not before, authority equal to God's, and that being equal is not being identical. John's Word who "was God" (John 1:1, BSB) is, on this account, the end of a development. | Secondhand authority mended. | bc | If a divine figure beside God was a Jewish idea before Jesus, then the earliest Christians didn't need Greek hero cults or three centuries to call Jesus divine; they could do it at once, in Jewish terms, which is what the letters show. In Galatians 4:14 the phrase "as if I were Christ Jesus Himself" reads more naturally as a step beyond "an angel of God" than as the same thing said twice. And Paul gives Christ a part in creation (1 Corinthians 8:6), which, as the note on John 1 summarizes Bauckham's and Hurtado's argument, Jewish monotheism reserved for God alone. Third, Jesus was given not only exalted titles but worship: prayer, the invoked name, the oath of Isaiah 45. | Length: the piece rose past 2,500 words with the Part 1 additions; tightened with no change of claim |

| bd | Bart Ehrman, in *How Jesus Became God* (2014), argues, as the Study Bible's note on John 1 records his view, that John's high view of Jesus is a late development, not what Jesus or his first followers believed. The divine claims in John, he argues, can't be traced to the historical Jesus by the tests historians use: they appear in only one source, the latest Gospel; they express exactly the view of Jesus that its author held; and there is no record of any Jew in Jesus' land ever saying such things about himself. If Jesus had gone about Galilee calling himself a divine being who existed before the world, Ehrman asks, how could Paul, Mark, the sayings Matthew and Luke share, and the material found only in Matthew or only in Luke all fail to mention it? The chapter title 'Did Jesus Think He Was God?' is as the reader names it. Paraphrased |
| bd | He calls himself "the Son of Man," and in Aramaic, as the note on Mark 2 records, "son of man" can simply mean a human being; Geza Vermes, in *Jesus the Jew* (1973), argued that it could be a modest way of saying "I," and Maurice Casey, in *Son of Man* (1979), that the references to Daniel were added by the early church. | He calls himself "the Son of Man," and in Aramaic, as the note on Mark 2 records, "son of man" can simply mean a human being; that note reports Geza Vermes's argument, in *Jesus the Jew* (1973), that it could be a modest way of saying "I," and the note on Daniel 7 reports Maurice Casey's, in *Son of Man* (1979), that the references to Daniel were added by the early church. | Secondhand authority marked: Vermes and Casey are now said to be the Notes on Mark 2 and Daniel 7's summaries (neither book reached); Casey's thesis that the Daniel references were added is in the Note on Daniel 7, not Mark 2, and is now attributed there |
| bd | (none) | Ehrman meets the obvious replies in advance. Jews of the time could speak of a king, of Moses or of Enoch as divine in some sense, he grants, but always as something others said of them, never as a claim made of oneself. Miracles don't show divinity, since Elijah and Elisha did them, and so, in Acts, did Peter and Paul. When Jesus forgives, he says, "Son, your sins are forgiven" (Mark 2:5, BSB), which, Ehrman argues, means that God has forgiven them, a declaration otherwise made by priests at the temple, so that Jesus may be claiming a priest's prerogative rather than God's. People knelt before kings as well as before God. And many such stories, on his view, are embellishments by later storytellers who had come to believe that Jesus was in some sense divine. In his own summary, Jesus thought of himself as a prophet of the coming end and as the future king of Israel in the age to come. | Ehrman reader: his answers to the usual arguments were missing. Checked and left. The Jewish reader: no sentence on the five pages says or implies that "the Jews" killed Jesus; the trial is framed as the Notes on Mark 14, Mark 15 and Matthew 27 frame it (some Jerusalem authorities took part; the decision and the execution were Roman; *Nostra Aetate*); Acts 2:36's "whom you crucified" is quoted as Scripture in a list of exaltation texts, and the Note on Acts 2 reads it as a word "placing the hearers inside the story", which the page does not contradict; the James passage in Josephus names the high priest and the citizens who objected. The two versions of Josephus's paragraph on Jesus are not used on any of the five pages. The classicist: Justin's *First Apology* 21, 22 and 33 and *Dialogue* 67 (ANF 1, anf01-46 and anf01-48), Celsus in Origen 1.28, 1.32 and 1.37 (Crombie), Suetonius, *Augustus* 94 (Thomson, revised by Forester), Josephus, *Antiquities* 20.9.1 (Whiston), 1 Clement 5 (ANF 1, anf01-05), Eusebius 2.23, 2.25 and 3.1 (McGiffert), Ignatius (ANF 1), Gibbon chapter 16 and its note, Schweitzer (Montgomery) and Russell were rechecked word for word by script; every translation is named on the page. The believer: the page on the disciples now speaks to the reader who was taught the fuller story.

Not reached, and therefore not used as primary: any work of Hurtado, Bauckham, Casey, Boyarin, Dunn, Brown or Lincoln; *How Jesus Became God* beyond the one section read aloud; Ehrman's argument that Paul himself thought Christ an angel (the brief names it; no source opened states it, so the page states the reading on its merits from Galatians 4:14 and does not put it in his mouth); the year of Lewis's essay.

## The area "authors": five more pieces (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/authors/` and `client/public/questions/authors/`: what-kind-of-writing-are-the-gospels.md, "bh"; the-synoptic-problem.md, "bi"; is-john-history-or-theology.md, "bj"; did-constantine-decide-the-bible.md, "bk"; did-paul-invent-christianity.md, "bl". From section 19's unwritten list for the area (the plan's items 3.4, 3.6, 3.7, 3.13 with 3.16, and 10.8, Paul being placed here as the brief directs). The area page lists them. Sources for this part, beyond those named above, all GH and public domain: "Plutarch" (the *Life of Alexander* in the translation under Dryden's name, two independent copies: `TheMITTech/classics`, `Plutarch/alexandr.1b.txt`, the Internet Classics Archive's text, "Translated by John Dryden"; and `Vast-As-A-Sea/English-version-of-world-famous-works`, `ALEXANDER.txt`, headed "356-323 B.C. ... translated by John Dryden"); "Thucydides" (*History of the Peloponnesian War*, Richard Crawley's translation, two copies: `Grobitalks/hearth-orbital-library`, `b/7142/0.txt`, Gutenberg #7142, title page "Translated by Richard Crawley"; and `mbykov/diglossa.data`, `Greek/Thucydides/The_Peloponnesian_War/Book_1.crawley`); "Suetonius" (Thomson, revised by Forester, the twenty-first run's copy); "Justin" (ANF 1, anf01-46, chapters LXVI and LXVII by the copy's own headings); "Eusebius" (McGiffert: books 2 and 3 as above; book 6 in `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `NPNF2-01/Npnf2-01-11.htm`, and a second copy in `scrollmapper/bible_databases_deuterocanonical`, `etc/en/church_history/nicene-2/eusebius-chuch-history-book-6/`); "Augustine" (*Harmony of the Gospels* 1.2, Salmond's translation in NPNF first series vol. 6, `gustavo-depaula/ember`, `content/books/church-fathers/augustine/harmony-of-the-gospels/en-US/ch002.md`, and `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `NPNF1-06/npnf1-06-12.htm`); "Chicago" (the Chicago Statement on Biblical Inerrancy, `NonlinearFruit/Creeds.json`, `creeds/chicago_statement_on_biblical_inerrancy.json`; Article XIII also quoted in the site's own `answers.ts`); "Guide" (the Study Bible's guide "Reading the Gospels", `client/public/bible/guides/reading-gospels.json`, and "How the Books Were Chosen", `guides/canon.json`); "Intro" (the Study Bible's book introductions, `client/public/bible/notes/<book>/intro.json`). Living scholars are paraphrased only from the guide or the notes, said so on the page, never quoted. Scripture is quoted from the BSB and checked by script; quotations with an ellipsis, or two verses under one reference, by hand.

### bh. "What kind of writing are the Gospels?" (what-kind-of-writing-are-the-gospels)

| Claim | How verified | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Schmidt, Dibelius and Bultmann (*The History of the Synoptic Tradition*, 1921): the Gospels "folk literature", units of oral tradition strung together by editors, no real parallel | Guide (section 1); the authors area's verified row for Bultmann's title and year (stories-changed-in-the-telling) | Paraphrased and marked as the guide's summary |
| Thucydides 1.22: "so my habit has been to make the speakers say what was in my opinion demanded of them by the various occasions"; "of course adhering as closely as possible to the general sense of what they really said"; "by the most severe and detailed tests possible" | Thucydides (two copies, Crawley) | Quoted |
| Plutarch, *Alexander* 1: "my design is not to write histories, but lives"; "epitomize the most celebrated parts"; "insist at large on every particular circumstance"; "the marks and indications of the souls of men"; "great battles to be treated of by others"; the thunderbolt dream, the serpent "found lying by Olympias as she slept", the rumor of a god; "Others say", "it is said" | Plutarch (two copies) | Quoted and paraphrased |
| Plutarch wrote some four centuries after Alexander's death | Plutarch (the copy's header: Alexander "356-323 B.C."; Plutarch "75 AD") | Stated as "some four centuries" |
| Suetonius, *Augustus* 9: "not in order of time, but arranging his acts into distinct classes" | Suetonius | Quoted |
| Matthew's five discourses and their closing formula | Intro (Matthew); Matthew 7:28 (BSB, script) | Paraphrased; quoted |
| Luke arranges "sometimes by theme rather than strict chronology" | Intro (Luke) | Quoted as the introduction's words |
| Papias: Mark wrote "accurately, though not in order" (*Church History* 3.39.15) | Eusebius (McGiffert, book 3) | Quoted |
| Talbert, *What Is a Gospel?* (1977); Burridge, *What Are the Gospels? A Comparison with Graeco-Roman Biography* (1992); the shared features; "of a distinctive Jewish and Christian kind"; Bauckham, *The Gospels for All Christians* (1998) | Guide (section 1) | Paraphrased and marked; the guide's phrase quoted as the guide's |
| Justin: "the memoirs composed by them, which are called Gospels" (*First Apology* 66); "the memoirs of the apostles or the writings of the prophets are read" (67) | Justin (ANF 1) | Quoted |
| Strauss, *Life of Jesus* (1835) treated much of the Gospels as myth | Guide (section 6); Intro (John) | Listed; the English title is not given (not in the sources read) |
| Chicago Statement (1978), Article XIII | Chicago (GH); `answers.ts` | Quoted |
| The argued answer "Why would anyone trust the Bible?": "Most scholars now read the Gospels as ancient biographies ... Burridge's *What Are the Gospels?* (1992)" | `answers.ts` | Agrees |
| Luke 1:2-3; John 20:30, 20:31; Matthew 7:28 | Repo (BSB; script, and by hand for the ellipsis in John 20:30) | Quoted |

# Twenty-third run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the five newest authors pieces, and five more pieces in the area "The Old Testament: history, archaeology, violence and law"

Method words as in the fourteenth to twenty-second runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, found with GitHub's code search (`gh search code`, paced) and fetched from raw.githubusercontent.com; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches. No WebSearch was run, so nothing below rests on a search summary. "Note", "Intro" and "Guide" are the Study Bible's notes, book introductions and guides in the repository, read this run for every chapter named. Every ancient text the brief named was fetched again this run from its GitHub copy (not read from an earlier run's scratch copy) into `/home/user/apol-scratch/r23/src/`:

- "PLUT": Plutarch, *Life of Alexander*, in the translation under Dryden's name, `TheMITTech/classics`, `Plutarch/alexandr.1b.txt` (the Internet Classics Archive's text).
- "THUC": Thucydides, *History of the Peloponnesian War*, book 1, Richard Crawley's translation, `mbykov/diglossa.data`, `Greek/Thucydides/The_Peloponnesian_War/Book_1.crawley`.
- "PHIL": Philostratus, *The Life of Apollonius of Tyana*, translated by F. C. Conybeare (Loeb, 1912), the sacred-texts edition in `jonnyg23/mahabrain`, `sacred_obsidian_vault/cla/aot/laot/laot00.md` to `laot03.md` (title page, introduction, book 1). New to the library.
- "JUSTIN": Justin, *First Apology*, ANF vol. 1 (`jennica/jennica.github.io`, `fathers/schaff/ANF-01/anf01-46.htm`), and the volume's introductory note (`anf01-45.htm`).
- "EUS 3", "EUS 6": Eusebius, *Church History*, McGiffert's translation, NPNF second series vol. 1, `Npnf2-01-08.htm` (book 3) and `Npnf2-01-11.htm` (book 6), same repository.
- "FL39": Athanasius, Festal Letter 39, "From Letter XXXIX. (for 367.)", NPNF second series vol. 4, `NPNF2-04/Npnf2-04-93.htm`.
- "MUR": the Muratorian fragment, ANF vol. 5, "Fragments of Caius", III, "Canon Muratorianus" (`ANF-05/anf05-134.htm`), with the volume's introductory notice (`anf05-133.htm`). New to the library: the twenty-second run read only the *Catholic Encyclopedia*'s description.
- "NIC": the twenty canons of Nicaea, NPNF second series vol. 14, `NPNF2-14/Npnf2-14-13.htm` to `-23.htm`, every canon read. New to the library beyond canon I.
- "VC": Eusebius, *Life of Constantine* 4.36, NPNF second series vol. 1, `NPNF2-01/Npnf2-01-30.htm`.

- "CE": the *Catholic Encyclopedia* (1907 to 1912) in `gustavo-depaula/ember`, `content/books/catholic-encyclopedia/en-US/`: 10642a (Muratorian Canon), 11044a (First Council of Nicaea; the copy stops after canon 11), 03274a (Canon of the New Testament), 02035a (Athanasius), 05617b (Eusebius), 11567b (St. Paul).
- "Schechter" and "Abrahams": the twentieth run's copies (GITenberg #48890 and #6971), re-read this run.

## The editor's read of the five newest authors pieces (changed facts and wording)

Read five ways, as the brief asked: as a classicist (every ancient source named with its translation and re-opened, nothing claimed of a text that could not be opened); as a Gospels scholar who holds the Farrer view and as one who holds Q (is each view stated so its holder would sign, and is "independent" used honestly?); as a historian of the canon (is anything implied about Hippo, Carthage or Athanasius beyond the sources, and are the Protestant, Catholic and Orthodox canons described evenly?); as a Jewish reader of the Paul page (Second Temple Judaism never a religion of earning, the law never a burden in a caricature); and as a logician (what each argument establishes, and any step that claims more). Every quotation on the five pages that is not Scripture was rechecked by script against the copies fetched this run (all match; the three with footnote numbers inside the quoted words, two in Eusebius and one in the Muratorian fragment, by hand); every BSB quotation by script, four with an ellipsis by hand. Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area page stay pending.

Where a living scholar's view rests on the site's own notes or guides, the page already said whose summary it is (Burridge and Talbert, "as the Study Bible's guide summarizes their work"; Goodacre, Kloppenborg and the solutions, "as the guide states them"; Sundberg and Hahneman, "as the Study Bible's guide to the canon records"; Sanders, "as the note on Luke 10 summarizes him"); the one place it didn't, Kruger on the Constantine page, now says "as the site's guide to the New Testament canon sets out the view Michael Kruger argues". Ehrman is given from the readings of his books and his interview the twenty-second run found, said so on each page.

Checked and left. PLUT: "my design is not to write histories, but lives", "epitomize the most celebrated parts", "insist at large on every particular circumstance", "the marks and indications of the souls of men", "great battles to be treated of by others", "Others say", "it is said": all match. THUC: "so my habit has been to make the speakers say what was in my opinion demanded of them by the various occasions", "of course adhering as closely as possible to the general sense of what they really said", "by the most severe and detailed tests possible": match. Suetonius 9 ("not in order of time, but arranging his acts into distinct classes"): match in the GITenberg copy of Thomson and Forester. JUSTIN 66 and 67 ("the memoirs composed by them, which are called Gospels"; "the memoirs of the apostles or the writings of the prophets are read"): match. EUS 3.39.15 (Papias, "accurately, though not in order"), 3.25 (the classes and their words): match. AUG 1.2 ("follows him closely, and looks like his attendant and epitomizer"): match; no translator is printed in the copy, so the page names the series. FL39 ("These are fountains of salvation ... Let no man add to these, neither let him take ought from these"): match, and the twenty-seven New Testament books are as the page says. VC 4.36: match. CE Nicaea: the conveyances and posts, Constantine "honorary president", Theonas and Secundus "exiled and anathematized", Easter and the Meletian schism, the three surviving pieces of the acts: match. CE Canon NT: "three councils there at brief intervals--Hippo, Carthage, in 393; Third of Carthage in 397; Carthage in 419--found it necessary to formulate catalogues"; the primitive East Syrian canon "did not contain any of the Catholic Epistles or Apocalypse"; "In St. John Chrysostom's ample expositions of the Scriptures there is not a single clear trace of the Apocalypse": match; the page's "at Hippo in 393 and at Carthage in 397 and 419" stands. CE Athanasius: exiled to Treves under Constantine in 336: match. Nothing on the Constantine page claims that Hippo or Carthage closed the canon for the whole church, that Athanasius's list was adopted anywhere, or that Nicaea discussed books; the "exactly" of the objection ("The first list that matches our New Testament exactly comes from 367") is the objection's own fact and is true of FL39. For the Jewish reader: the Paul page nowhere calls the law a burden; the Antioch quarrel is about table fellowship; "the dispute Paul fought was over whether Gentiles must become Jews" frames the law as Israel's, not as a yoke Jesus lifted; Paul is called a Jew in his own words (Romans 11:1).

Not reached, and therefore not used: Wrede's book itself; Streeter's book; Burridge's, Talbert's, Goodacre's and Kruger's books (each given as the site's guide gives them); any text of the councils of Hippo or Carthage (described only as CE and the guides describe them).

## The area "old-testament": five more pieces (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/old-testament/` and `client/public/questions/old-testament/`: who-wrote-the-first-five-books.md, "bm"; the-god-of-the-old-testament.md, "bn"; which-old-testament-laws.md, "bo"; abraham-and-isaac.md, "bp"; what-archaeology-shows.md, "bq". From section 19's unwritten list for the area (the plan's items 6.6, 6.12, 6.13, 6.17, and 6.7 with 6.8). The area page lists them. Ancient and older texts are quoted only from public-domain translations fetched this run from GitHub copies; living scholars are given only as the Study Bible's notes, introductions and guides, or a named reference entry, give them, said so on the page; no consensus is given as a percentage or head-count. Scripture is quoted from the BSB and checked by script; quotations with an ellipsis, or with two verses under one reference, by hand.

Sources new to this part, all GH: "SPIN" (Spinoza, *A Theological-Political Treatise*, part II, R. H. M. Elwes's translation, GITenberg `Theologico-Political-Treatise-Part-2_990`, chapter VIII); "WELL" (Wellhausen, *Prolegomena to the History of Israel*, translated by J. Sutherland Black and Allan Menzies, GITenberg `Prolegomena-to-the-History-of-Israel_4732`; the preface: the work "first appeared in 1878", "the second edition ... appeared in 1883 ... under the new title of 'Prolegomena'"); "CE Pent" (*Catholic Encyclopedia*, "Pentateuch", 11646c, `gustavo-depaula/ember`); "CE Marc" ("Marcionites", 09645c, same); "PRAV" (the *Orthodox Encyclopedia* (Pravoslavnaya Entsiklopediya), article on Umberto Cassuto, `slavonic/pravenc-md`, `articles/1681295.md`, a named reference entry, for the English title of his lectures).

### bm. "Weren't the first five books written by many hands, centuries after Moses?" (who-wrote-the-first-five-books)

| Claim | How verified | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Spinoza, 1670, chapter 8: Ibn Ezra's "dark hints"; Deuteronomy 31:9 "words that cannot be ascribed to Moses"; Genesis 12:6 "thus clearly excluding the time at which he wrote"; Numbers 12:3; Moses' death and Deuteronomy 34:10; "the plain conclusion that these books were written by another, and not by Moses in person" | SPIN (lines 1540 to 1700 of the copy), read this run | Quoted (the conclusion) and paraphrased; the year 1670 from the Intro (Genesis) |
| Astruc 1753, "a French doctor", divine names, "He suggested Moses had used separate sources"; J, E, D, P; Wellhausen 1878 and 1883; Van Seters (1975), Schmid and Rendtorff (1977) "questioned the old theory"; "Scholars have not agreed on what should replace it"; priestly and non-priestly; a revised four-source view still has defenders; Cassuto, Hebrew 1941, English 1961, "God" for the Creator, "the LORD" for the God who comes near; "Christians who confess the Bible as God's word hold more than one of these views"; "These notes do not settle the question, and the book's authority does not hang on knowing the writer's name"; "A few details show at least some later updating, which many conservative scholars readily accept"; Dan (Genesis 14:14, Judges 18:29); Edom's kings (Genesis 36:31) | Intro (Genesis), read this run | Quoted where marked, otherwise paraphrased, said to be the introduction's |

# Twenty-fourth run (2026-11-20): an independent editor's read of the five newest old-testament pieces, and five more pieces in the area "Science"

Method words as in the fourteenth to twenty-third runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository and fetched this run from raw.githubusercontent.com or by a shallow `git clone`; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches. GitHub's code search did not answer this run (the session's `gh` token was refused), so every source below was reached by a path an earlier run recorded or by cloning a repository whose name was known and listing its files. No WebSearch was run, so nothing below rests on a search summary. Every source used was fetched again this run into `/home/user/apol-scratch/r24/src/`; none was read from an earlier run's scratch copy. "Note", "Intro" and "Guide" are the Study Bible's notes, book introductions and guides in the repository, dumped and read this run for every chapter named.

Sources fetched again for Part 1, all GH: Sayce, *Fresh Light from the Ancient Monuments* (2nd ed., 1884; `GITenberg/Fresh-Light-from-the-Ancient-Monuments--13-A-Sketch-of-the-Most-Striking-Confirmations-of-the__32883`, 32883-0.txt); the *Aquifer Open Bible Dictionary* ("AQ", an adaptation under CC BY-SA 4.0 of the *Tyndale Open Bible Dictionary*, the copy's licence line "© 2023 Tyndale House Publishers"; `BibleAquifer/AquiferOpenBibleDictionary`, `eng/md/*.content.md`, cloned this run): entries "Moabite Stone", "Hezekiah", "Sennacherib", "Black Obelisk", "Shalmaneser", "Siloam Inscription", "Shishak"; Irenaeus, *Against Heresies* 1.27 and 5.25 (ANF vol. 1, `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `fathers/schaff/ANF-01/anf01-58.htm`, `anf01-63.htm`); Tertullian, *Against Marcion* 1 (`ANF-03/anf03-28.htm`); Justin, *Dialogue with Trypho* (`ANF-01/anf01-48.htm`); the *Catholic Encyclopedia*'s "Marcionites" (09645c) and "Pentateuch" (11646c) (`gustavo-depaula/ember`); Aquinas I-II q.99 (`Geremia/AquinasOperaOmnia`, `english/summa/FS/FS099.html`); the Westminster Confession (`NonlinearFruit/Creeds.json`); the Formula of Concord, Epitome (`Zyniker13/Denominations-and-Traditions`); Kant's *Streit der Fakultäten* (`emilianotsalomon/idealisms-working-group`); Singer's *Standard Prayer Book* (`jonnyg23/mahabrain`, `jud/spb/spb34.md`, `spb23.md`); the *Orthodox Encyclopedia* on Harnack (`slavonic/pravenc-md`, `articles/161705.md`); two of the three Dawkins copies (`devnall/notes`, `QuoteFile.txt`; `openprivacy/fennet`, `_quotes/2003-06-13-119.md`); Spinoza (`GITenberg/Theologico-Political-Treatise-Part-2_990`, 990.txt); Wellhausen (`GITenberg/Prolegomena-to-the-History-of-Israel_4732`, 4732.txt); Schechter, *Studies in Judaism* (`GITenberg/Studies-in-Judaism-First-Series_48890`, 48890-0.txt); Every non-Scripture quotation on the five pages was rechecked by script against these copies (all match; Tertullian 1.6, with the copy's footnote numbers inside the words, and the CE's "still ... upheld", with an ellipsis, by hand). Every BSB quotation was rechecked by script, the two with an ellipsis (Exodus 19:5-6, Romans 13:9-10) by hand.

## The editor's read of the five newest old-testament pieces (changed facts and wording)

Read five ways, as the brief asked: as a rabbi (is the Akedah described as Jewish tradition holds it; is the Talmud line traceable to a named translation; is the "angry God" page free of the contrast it rejects; is Torah ever "law" as burden?); as a pentateuchal critic and as a defender of Mosaic authorship (would each sign the statement of his view?); as an archaeologist (each inscription's content, date and find-place opened in a named source this run; Sayce's 1884 readings marked as dated, nothing resting on them alone); as a survivor of religious abuse reading the Abraham page (the care passage near the top and the end; no sentence asking for obedience against conscience); and as a logician (what each argument establishes). Every change is made in the Markdown record and the data (`diff-questions-record.mjs` at 0). All five and the area page stay pending.

| Piece | Before | After | Why, and how verified |
|---|---|---|---|
| bp | The story teaches trust in God's promise when it seems contradicted. It gives no one permission to harm anyone, and it never has. | The story is about trusting the God who made the promise; it never asks anyone to act against conscience, and it gives no one permission to harm anyone. | Survivor of religious abuse: 'trust in God's promise when it seems contradicted' can be heard as a call to obey against conscience, the pattern abusers use. Now the short answer says plainly the story asks no one to act against conscience. 'and it never has' cut: the logician's point below (no one can show how every reader in every age used it) |
| bp | In the Talmud, Rabbi Abbahu explains why the ram's horn is blown: "Said the Holy One: blow before me with a ram's horn in order that I remember for you the binding of Isaac son of Abraham, and I will consider it as if you bound yourselves before me" (Rosh Hashanah 16a, in the English text of the Sefaria library). | In the Talmud, Rabbi Abbahu asks why the horn blown at the New Year is a ram's horn, and answers in God's name: so that God may remember the binding of Isaac, son of Abraham, and count it to Israel as though they had shown the same readiness themselves (Rosh Hashanah 16a, given here in paraphrase; Michael Rodkinson's English translation, in the edition of 1918, renders the passage in full). | Rabbi: the quotation came from Sefaria's merged English file, which combines two translations without saying which line is whose, so it could not be traced to a named translation. Now paraphrased and said to be paraphrase; Rodkinson's translation (Tract Rosh Hashana, chapter I, sacred-texts edition dated 1918, GH `jonnyg23/mahabrain`, `sacred_obsidian_vault/jud/t02/ros03.md`) read this run: 'R. Abbahu said: "Why is the cornet made a ram's horn?" ... that I may remember, for your sake, the offering of Isaac, the son of Abraham ... and I shall consider even you as worthy, as if ye had shown an equal readiness'. 'Binding' follows the page's own word Akedah; 'readiness' follows Rodkinson |
| bp | 3. Read within those Scriptures, the story teaches trust in God's promise and his provision of a substitute, and both the Jewish and the Christian traditions that keep it have read it that way, never as a warrant to harm a child. | 3. Read within those Scriptures, the story teaches trust in God's promise and his provision of a substitute, and the Jewish and Christian traditions that keep it read it that way in their prayers and creeds, not as a warrant to harm a child. | Logician: 'have read it that way, never as a warrant' was a universal claim about every reader in two traditions over three thousand years, which no source can establish; now it claims what the evidence shows (the liturgy and the confessions quoted on the page) |
| bp | It is trust in a known God whose goodness Abraham had reason to rely on, and the chapter vindicates that trust, not the act of killing. | It is trust in a known God whose goodness Abraham had reason to rely on, and the chapter vindicates that trust, not the act of killing. Nor does the chapter ask any reader to do what Abraham was stopped from doing, or to obey a voice, a leader or a feeling against conscience. The God of this chapter is the one who forbade the act. | Survivor of religious abuse: the reply defended Abraham's trust without saying, at that point, that no reader is asked to obey against conscience; now it does |
| bp | All of them confess that God does not desire the death of children, that he provided the sacrifice he asked for, and that in Jesus Christ he gave his own Son. | All of them confess that God does not desire the death of children, that he provided the sacrifice he asked for, and that in Jesus Christ he gave his own Son. If this page has stirred fear for yourself or for a child, tell someone you trust today; if a child is in danger, call the police; and Find Help (/help) lists places to turn now. | Survivor of religious abuse: the care paragraph stood in the middle of the page (the end of the evidence) and the short answer at the top; the page's last section had none. Irenaeus, Against Heresies 1.27.1-2 (ANF vol. 1, GH `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `fathers/schaff/ANF-01/anf01-58.htm`, fetched this run): 'Cerdo ... taught that the God proclaimed by the law and the prophets was not the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ ... Marcion of Pontus succeeded him, and developed his doctrine' |

Schechter, Studies in Judaism (1896), GITenberg #48890 (`GITenberg/Studies-in-Judaism-First-Series_48890`, 48890-0.txt), fetched this run: 'proclaiming ex cathedra, that the Law was a most terrible burden ... On the other side we have the testimony of a literature extending over about twenty-five centuries ... give unanimous evidence in favour of this Law, and of the bliss and happiness of living and dying under it' |

Rabbi: the sentence could be heard as saying mercy and justice were unreconciled until the cross; it now first says Jewish tradition holds them together in its own way |

| bo | The objection says that this is not interpretation but preference. | The objection says, in the form it is commonly put (no single published author is named for it here), that this is not interpretation but preference. | The brief: the modern form of the objection has no published modern objector in a readable source (none was found; code search does not answer this run), so the page now says plainly that it is given in its common form; Trypho, below, remains its oldest written form |

Those who keep it have not experienced it as the burden Christians have sometimes described. Solomon Schechter, in *Studies in Judaism* (1896), answered the scholars who called it "a most terrible burden" by pointing to a literature of some twenty-five centuries that bears witness to "the bliss and happiness of living and dying under it." | Rabbi: the page quoted 'not to burden you' (Acts 15:28) and the Lutheran 'curse' without Jewish witness to the Torah as joy; Schechter's sentence (GITenberg #48890, fetched this run) now stands in the concessions |
| bo | and the reasons it gives, holiness of heart rather than of food, the shadow and the body, are in the texts, not invented by medieval theologians. | and the reasons it gives, that God "made no distinction between us and them, for He cleansed their hearts by faith" (Acts 15:9, BSB), and the shadow and the body, are in the texts, not invented by medieval theologians. | Rabbi: 'holiness of heart rather than of food' set an inward Christian holiness against an outward Jewish one, the old contrast the page elsewhere refuses; the reason is now given in the council's own words (Acts 15:9, BSB, checked) |
| bo | So: Christians who don't keep the food laws or the laws of mixtures but do keep the commands of love are following a principle the New Testament gives, not choosing at random; | So: Christians who don't keep the food laws or the laws of mixtures but do keep the commands of love are following a principle the New Testament gives, not a preference of their own; | Logician: the objection charged preference, not randomness; 'not choosing at random' answered a weaker charge than the one stated |
| bm | The critic's own view has a debt to pay as well. Wellhausen's reconstruction rested on reading the history of Israel's worship as a straight line from many altars to one, and the decades since have not agreed on what replaces it. What they show is that the sources and their dates are still argued, which is a reason for modesty about any reconstruction, not a verdict for either side. | Pentateuchal critic: the paragraph implied that the field's revisions weakened the critical case in general; the Genesis introduction (re-read this run) says they questioned 'especially its early date for the J source', that is, they moved dates later. A critic can now sign the sentence. 'a straight line' softened to 'a line' (Wellhausen's argument, read this run in WELL, is about the order of the codes, not a claim of a straight development) |
| bm | - Baruch Spinoza, *Theological-Political Treatise* (1670), chapter 8, the first full argument against Moses' authorship of the whole. | - Baruch Spinoza, *Theological-Political Treatise* (1670), chapter 8, an early full argument against Moses' authorship of the whole. | Historian: 'the first' is a priority claim no source opened supports (Spinoza himself builds on Ibn Ezra, and others wrote on the question in the same century); now 'an early' |
| bq | Mesha even reads his history as Israel read its own, in terms of his god's anger and favor. | Mesha even reads his history as Israel read its own, in terms of his god's anger and favor. Sayce's translations are of 1884, and later scholars have revised many readings in these inscriptions, so this page rests nothing on his wording alone: for each line quoted from him, the sense is also given by a modern reference work, the *Aquifer Open Bible Dictionary* (an adaptation of the *Tyndale Open Bible Dictionary*), or by the Study Bible's notes. Sayce himself, for instance, put the stone's discovery in 1869; the notes and the dictionary give 1868. | Archaeologist: the page used Sayce's 1884 translations without saying on the page that they are dated. Now it says so, and every factual claim resting on a Sayce line has a second source: AQ (`BibleAquifer/AquiferOpenBibleDictionary`, eng/md, cloned this run) gives Mesha's Omri 'oppressed Moab for many days because Chemosh was angry', Omri's son 'I will oppress Moab', 46 towns, 'shut up in Jerusalem like a bird in a cage', '800 talents according to Assyrian records' and 30 of gold, and Klein's report of 19 August 1868; the notes on 2 Kings 3, 18, 19 and 20 give the rest. Sayce's 1869 (s.txt line 1923) is the one reading of his the later sources correct |
| bq | then they were historians of a kind, not inventors, and their work deserves to be read as ancient history is read: critically, but not with the assumption that whatever can't be checked was made up. | then where they can be checked they behave as historians of a kind, and their work deserves to be read as ancient history is read: critically, but not with the assumption that whatever can't be checked was made up. | Logician: 'historians of a kind, not inventors' claimed more than the reply's premise allows (good records where checked don't rule out invention where unchecked); now the claim is limited to where they can be checked |

Checked and left. The archaeologist: every inscription's content, date and find-place on the archaeology page was opened this run. Tel Dan (main fragment 1993, two more 1994, northern Israel, "house of David", bytdwd; Khirbet Qeiyafa excavated from 2007): Note (2 Samuel 7). Mesha (black basalt, Dhiban, 1868, the Louvre): Note (2 Kings 3) and AQ "Moabite Stone" (Klein's report of 19 August 1868); Sayce gives 1869, now said on the page. Kurkh Monolith (Ahab with a large force of chariots at Qarqar, 853 BC): Notes (1 Kings 16 and 22) and AQ "Shalmaneser" ("the battle of Qarqar (853 BC) ... supported by King Ahab of Israel"). Black Obelisk (Nimrud, 1846, a figure bowing, caption naming Jehu): Note (2 Kings 9) and AQ "Black Obelisk" ("King Jehu ... bowing before Shalmaneser"). Sennacherib's prisms (forty-six cities, "like a bird in a cage", thirty talents of gold, the silver higher; the city never claimed taken): Notes (2 Kings 18 and 19, Isaiah 36, 2 Chronicles 32) and AQ "Hezekiah" ("800 talents according to Assyrian records") and "Sennacherib" ("46 other towns"); Sayce's wording for each matches the copy. The Siloam inscription (found in 1880; the two crews met): Notes (2 Kings 20, 2 Chronicles 32) and Sayce (who also gives the summer of 1880). Sheshonq I's relief at Karnak: Note (1 Kings 14) and AQ "Shishak". Warren's Shaft (1867) and the stepped stone structure: Note (2 Samuel 5). No Sayce reading carries a claim alone. The pentateuchal critic and the defender: the objection's statement of the critical view (Spinoza's seams, Astruc, J, E, D and P, Wellhausen's order of the codes, an anthology assembled after the exile) was checked against SPIN, WELL and the Intro and left; the defender's view (Moses the substantial author with later updating, the 1906 answers, Jesus' "Moses wrote" as asserting authorship) is stated in its own terms in the weight paragraph and the "Where Christians differ" section, and was left. The rabbi: the Akedah is described from the New Year liturgy in Singer's translation and the *Ethics of the Fathers*, as a plea for mercy and a trial Abraham stood, which is how those texts hold it; nothing on the five pages calls the Torah a burden in the page's own voice (Acts 15:28's "not to burden you" is Scripture about the council's requirements, and the Formula of Concord's "curse" is given as Lutheran teaching); the "angry God" page no longer has a sentence that leaves mercy and justice unreconciled before the cross. The survivor: the Abraham page names no means of harm, asks no one to forgive, trust or return, and now has a care line at its top, in its middle and at its end. The logician: the archaeology, laws and God pages' arguments were each narrowed where a step or the reply claimed more than the steps show (rows above); the Pentateuch argument's fourth step claims only a possible reading, and its conclusion only that the evidence settles neither extreme, and was left.

Not reached, and therefore not used: Sefaria's William Davidson (Steinsaltz) English for Rosh Hashanah 16a as a separate, named file (the Sefaria export repository holds no texts; the data repository's research tree is too large to search without code search); any text of the Tel Dan stele or the Kurkh Monolith beyond the notes' and AQ's descriptions.

## The area "science": five more pieces (pieces and keys)

Pieces: `docs/work/elite/apol-pilot/science/` and `client/public/questions/science/`: how-old-is-the-earth.md, "br"; adam-eve-and-human-origins.md, "bs"; is-the-mind-just-the-brain.md, "bt"; neuroscience-and-free-will.md, "bu"; near-death-experiences.md, "bv". From section 19's unwritten list for the area (the plan's items 7.4, 7.6, 7.7, 7.8 and 7.13). The area page lists them. Sources new to this part, all GH, fetched this run: "SEP" entries in `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/*.txt` (a copy of the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy; each entry's first-published and revision dates are printed at its head and given below), cloned this run; "OSA" (OpenStax, *Astronomy 2e*, `openstax/osbooks-astronomy`, modules m59820 "Dating Planetary Surfaces" and m59872 "Meteorites: Stones from Heaven"; the repository gives no year, so none is given on the page); "OSB" (OpenStax, *Biology 2e*, `openstax/osbooks-biology-bundle`, module m66594 "The Evolution of Primates", listed in `collections/biology-2e.collection.xml`); "WHITE" (Andrew Dickson White, *A History of the Warfare of Science with Theology in Christendom*, `GITenberg/History-of-the-Warfare-of-Science-with-Theology-in-Christendom_505`, 505.txt, "Two Volumes Combined"; its prefaces are dated 14 April 1894 and 15 August 1895, and the year of publication, 1896, is the one the science area's page already gives from an earlier verified row); "CITY" (Augustine, *City of God*, book 18, *Nicene and Post-Nicene Fathers*, first series, vol. 2, `jennica/jennica.github.io`, `fathers/schaff/NPNF1-02/npnf1-02-24.htm`, its heading "Book XVIII"). Living scientists and scholars are paraphrased only from these reference works or the Study Bible's notes, said so on the page; none is quoted. No consensus is given as a percentage or head-count. Scripture is quoted from the BSB and checked by script.

(it takes no side, calls the question one where faithful Christians differ, and gives the same readings and difficulties; the page follows it and adds the science and the genealogies), the science area's page on evolution (the same readings, the same confession), and the Study Bible's notes on Genesis 1 and 5. ### bt. "Hasn't science shown that the mind is just the brain?" (is-the-mind-just-the-brain)

| Claim | How verified | Result |
|---|---|---|
| "Conscious experience in humans depends on brain activity"; neural correlates of consciousness (Crick and Koch 1998, 2003); identifying correlates "is an important first step in understanding consciousness, but it is an early step", "correlates are not necessarily explanatory"; Chalmers (1995, 1996): empirical explanations "fundamentally either structural or functional, yet phenomenal consciousness is not reducible to either", "something that is left out"; the explanatory gap (Levine 1983); "Perhaps the most common attitude for neuroscientists is to set the hard problem aside"; Dennett (2018) denies the hard problem | SEP "The Neuroscience of Consciousness" (2018), sections 1.5 and 1.6, read this run | Quoted where marked; Chalmers, a living author, paraphrased only from the entry's account, never quoted |
| The "easy problems" and the hard problem (Chalmers 1995); optimistic physicalists (Dennett 1991) see present gaps as "the early stage of inquiry"; the gap "may simply reflect the limits of our current theorizing" (Dennett 1991); explanatory gaps "to be expected" on some physicalist views; "Discussion and disagreement on these topics remains active and ongoing"; Dennett, *Consciousness Explained* (1991), and Crick, *The Astonishing Hypothesis: The Scientific Search for the Soul* (1994), in the bibliography | SEP "Consciousness" (2004, rev. 2014), sections 5.2 and 5.4 and bibliography | Quoted and paraphrased; Dennett paraphrased as the entry summarizes him; Crick listed by title and year only (no claim of his is stated on the page) |

### bu. "Hasn't neuroscience shown that free will is an illusion?" (neuroscience-and-free-will)

| Libet et al. (1983): W "200 milliseconds before the motor act, but 350-400 milliseconds after readiness potential (RP)", RP "a ramp-like buildup of electrical activity that occurs in the brain and precedes actual movement"; Libet and others concluded "the conscious intention or decision to move cannot be the true cause of action because it comes too late"; Mele (2009): the RP "need not be construed as the cause of the action but rather is best interpreted as the beginning of forming an intention to act"; other critics (Levy, Nahmias); objections "have led many contemporary philosophers (including many skeptics) to reject the neuroscientific argument"; "the causes that move us are often less transparent to ourselves than we might assume"; higher processes "can and often do occur in the absence of conscious choice and guidance"; implicit biases; some compatibilists grant free will "at best an occasional phenomenon" (Baumeister 2008); skeptics about basic desert; bibliography for Libet 1983 (*Brain* 106(3)), Libet 1985, Wegner 2002 (MIT Press), Mele 2009 (*Effective Intentions*, Oxford) | SEP "Skepticism About Moral Responsibility" (2018), section 2.5 and bibliography | Quoted where marked; Mele and Baumeister, living, given only as the entry gives them |

Habermas's "evidential aspect" and its three kinds; Moreland and Habermas (1998); Augustine (2008) "One of the most thorough discussions"; living persons met; EEG may not reveal all activity, timing; inaccurate reports; "stories often repeated tend to gain new features of interest in the telling"; hearing while apparently unconscious; Augustine "makes little effort to establish that the factors cited in his naturalistic explanations were actually operative"; "we may be close to an impasse"; "it seems unlikely that the fundamental disagreements can be resolved"; bibliography: Moreland and Habermas, *Beyond Death: Exploring the Evidence for Immortality* (Crossway, 1998); van Lommel, van Wees, Meyers and Elfferich, "Near-Death Experiences in Survivors of Cardiac Arrest: A Prospective Study in the Netherlands", *Lancet* (2001); Zaleski, *Otherworld Journeys: Accounts of Near-Death Experiences in Medieval and Modern Times* (1987); At the end of the argued text: "If this page has stirred thoughts of death that frighten you, or thoughts of ending your life, the site's Find Help page (/help) lists people you can reach today, and you should reach them." # Twenty-fifth run (2026-11-20): nine areas go live, and five more pieces in the area "Morality, sexuality and the church's record"

Method words as in the fourteenth to twenty-fourth runs. "GH" is a file read from a public GitHub repository, fetched this run from raw.githubusercontent.com or by a `git clone` without blobs and `git show` of the one file; GitHub is still the only source host this machine reaches. GitHub's code search answered this run and was used only to find a path; nothing below rests on a search result's summary. No WebSearch was run. "SEP <title>" is an entry of the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy read in the copy `tylermarcuscross/philosophers-gpt`, `entries/<title>.txt`, with the dates printed at its head. "HCF" is the public-domain library `HistoricalChristianFaith/Writings-Database` (the *Ante-Nicene Fathers* and *Nicene and Post-Nicene Fathers* translations, one file per work). Keys: "bw", "bx", "by", "bz", "ca".

### bz. "Is Christian nationalism Christian?" (is-christian-nationalism-christian)

Sources: this library's live pages on the Inquisition and on whether religion poisons everything (their rows "at" and "aw", for Tertullian, Lactantius, Augustine, the Donatists, Manz, Hubmaier, Williams, the Peace of Augsburg, the wars of religion and the Catholic teaching of 1965); the site's live pages `/nation/empire` ("The Danger of Empire") and `/nation/christian-nation` ("Was America Founded as a Christian Nation?") and `/topics/faith-politics-and-the-flag`; "WCF" (the Westminster Confession, `NonlinearFruit/Creeds.json`, the copy fetched in the twenty-fourth run, chapter 23, section 3, in the original wording); "RW" (a critic's essay that quotes Wolfe's definition with its page, `reformed-witness/confessions-of-grace`, `src/main/resources/seed/posts/christian_nationalism.md`, opened this run).

| Claim | How verified | Result |
|---|---|---|
| Wolfe (2022) defines Christian nationalism as "a totality of national action, consisting of civil laws and social customs, conducted by a Christian nation as a Christian nation, in order to procure for itself both earthly and heavenly good in Christ" (p. 9) | RW, which quotes the sentence with its page; the book's title and year from the site's essay ledger `what-christian-nationalism-is-and-is-not.md` (search-checked there) | Paraphrased, said on the page to be a passage one of his critics quotes; Wolfe (living) not quoted; the book was not opened |
| Westminster 23.3 (1646): "he hath authority, and it is his duty to take order, that unity and peace be preserved in the Church, that the truth of God be kept pure and entire, that all blasphemies and heresies be suppressed" | WCF | Quoted |
| Ambrose barred Theodosius from the table in 390 until he did public penance for the massacre at Thessalonica; the Edict of Milan, 313; Theodosius made Nicene Christianity the official religion of the empire in 380 | `/nation/empire` | Paraphrased, said to be the site's essay's account |
| Tertullian: "It is assuredly no part of religion to compel religion"; Lactantius: "religion is to be defended, not by putting to death, but by dying" | Rows under "at" and "aw" (ANF) | Quoted as the live pages quote them |
| Imperial law suppressed the Donatists from 405; Augustine once opposed coercion and came to defend it with "compel them to come in" (Luke 14:23) | The morality area page (its words) and row "at" | Paraphrased |
| Felix Manz drowned in Zurich in 1527 for baptizing adults; the Peace of Augsburg and the ruler's right to choose; the French wars of religion 1562 to 1598; the Thirty Years' War 1618 to 1648 | Rows "at" and "aw"; the area page | Paraphrased; no year is given for Augsburg, as on the live page |
| Hubmaier, *Concerning Heretics and Those Who Burn Them* (1524); Williams, *The Bloudy Tenent of Persecution* (1644), rulers to leave dissenters and unbelievers alone until God's harvest; the Catholic Church in 1965, no one may be forced to believe | Row "at" (Note on Matthew 13) | Paraphrased |
| New England Baptists "taxed to support other men's churches and jailed for preaching without a license"; Isaac Backus fought the Congregationalist establishment, on the conviction "that a faith the magistrate can compel is a faith already dead" | `/nation/christian-nation` | Paraphrased, said to be the site's essay's account; told in the third person |

Agreement: the two live morality pages, the area page's definition of Christian nationalism, and the site's topic page and nation essays.
